[wbe29d1-121] TIPSS technique A controlled study comparing TIPSS with 8 mm and 10 mm covered stent was stopped early as the 8 mm stent was not effective in controlling portal hypertensive complications (variceal bleeding and ascites). 210 The rate of hepatic encephalopathy was equal between the two groups. However, in another study of variceal bleeding, 8 mm stents were as effective as 10 mm stents in preventing rebleeding, with a 50% reduction in the incidence of encephalopathy with 8 mm stents. 211 Nonetheless, the applicability of these data in patients with refractory ascites is unclear, and evidence suggests that stent diameter increases over time. 212 In a retrospective study of patients with refractory ascites, 10 mm covered stent led to better control of ascites than an 8 mm stent, without any increase in encephalopathy. 213 Data from recent TIPSS registry in Germany reported an improved survival with 8 mm than with 10 mm covered stent. 214 The optimal diameter of covered TIPS stent in refractory ascites remains unclear.

[wbe29d1-122] The volume of TIPSS being performed in an individual hospital influences outcomes. Inpatient mortality was significantly lower in centres performing more than 20 TIPS procedures per year. 215 Patient selection Careful selection of patients with refractory ascites to be treated with TIPSS is vital to maximise the benefits and reduce the harmful effects of the treatment. The exclusion criteria for the insertion of TIPSS in the seven RCTs are reported in online supplemental table 10 .

[wbe29d1-123] Patients with advanced stages of cirrhosis have been excluded from the trials as indicated by serum bilirubin, 197–202 prolonged prothrombin time, 198 199 renal dysfunction 194 197 198 200–202 and Child-Pugh score. 200–202 Presence of chronic hepatic encephalopathy was an exclusion criterion in all the RCTs; the presence of pre-TIPS encephalopathy has been shown to be a predictor of poor outcome following TIPSS. 216–218

[wbe29d1-124] In retrospective studies, patients with advanced disease had a worse outcome following TIPS insertion. The MELD score was originally developed to predict survival following TIPSS and included serum creatinine, bilirubin, INR and aetiology of cirrhosis. 219 The MELD score has now evolved into a prognostic score in patients with cirrhosis. 220 Among patients with refractory ascites treated with TIPSS with covered stent, 1-year survival is 84% in those with MELD score <15 compared with 54% for MELD score >18. 221 In refractory ascites, a simple model of serum bilirubin and platelet count has been shown to predict 1-year survival. 222 The survival rate of patients with serum bilirubin >50 µmol/L or platelet count of <75×10 9 /L was only 31.2% compared with 73.1% in patients with serum bilirubin <50 µmol/L and platelet count of >75×10 9 /L. The exact survival rate quoted here has to be interpreted cautiously as earlier patients in the cohort were treated with a bare metal stent before the introduction of a PTFE-covered stent. Hyponatraemia has also been shown to be related poor survival following TIPSS, 223 but subsequent studies have not reproduced this finding. 221 224 Up to 50% of patients with decompensated cirrhosis have sarcopenia, which in turn negatively affects clinical outcome in these patients. However, the direct impact of sarcopenia on outcomes following TIPSS is unclear. Sarcopenia has been shown to be independently associated with development of post-TIPSS encephalopathy, but this study included patients with refractory ascites and uncontrolled variceal bleeding. 225 A recent retrospective study published in abstract form on patients undergoing TIPSS for refractory ascites reported no impact of sarcopenia on outcomes after TIPSS. 226 Furthermore, they reported an improvement in muscle mass following TIPSS insertion.

[wbe29d1-125] Although increasing age has been associated with poorer survival following TIPSS, 215 227 228 the mean age in these studies was between 54 and 57 years. Patients above the age of 70 were generally excluded from the previously mentioned RCTs. There is a lack of data in the literature for clinical outcome following TIPS in older patients (>70 years). Interestingly, functional disability as measured by patient-reported activities of daily living predicts post-TIPSS mortality adjusted for MELD score. 229 The management of hepatic encephalopathy after TIPSS is beyond the scope of this document and is discussed in the recent BSG/BASL TIPSS guidelines.

[wbe29d1-126] Recommendations TIPSS should be considered in patients with refractory ascites. ( Quality of evidence: high; Recommendation: strong ) Caution is required if considering TIPSS in patients with age >70 years, serum bilirubin >50 µmol/L, platelet count <75×10 9 /L, MELD core ≥18, current hepatic encephalopathy, active infection or hepatorenal syndrome. ( Quality of evidence: moderate; Recommendation: strong )

[wbe29d1-127] Management of umbilical hernia in patients with ascites In the cirrhotic patient, the incidence of abdominal wall hernia is 16% and reaches 24% in the presence of ascites; more than half of these are umbilical hernias. 230 These progressively enlarge and are prone to complications, including ulceration of the overlying skin, incarceration, strangulation, and rupture. Non-operative management of complicated hernias with antibiotics and dressing changes might result in mortality rates in the range of 60–88%. 231 On the other hand, hernia repair in cirrhosis has often been avoided due to high postoperative morbidity and mortality. Emergency surgery (OR=10.32, 95% CI 3.66 to 47.82), Child-Pugh-Turcotte class C (OR=5.52, 1.67 to 32.45), American Society of Anaesthesiologists (ASA) score ≥3 (OR=8.65, 3.65 to 87.23) and MELD score ≥20 (OR=2.15, 2.71 to 32.68) are associated with mortality. 232

[wbe29d1-128] A recent retrospective series of 102 patients, who underwent surgical repair of the umbilical hernia in the presence of ascites, included 45 patients having emergency surgery (24 with incarceration, 12 with rupture of the hernia sac, and nine with skin ulceration or necrosis). Morbidity and mortality rates of 37.2% and 3.9%, respectively, were observed. 233 Optimising management of ascites, including LVP and TIPSS perioperatively, could reduce the risk of wound dehiscence and recurrence of hernia. 234

[wbe29d1-129] Recommendations Suitability and timing of surgical repair of umbilical hernia should be considered in discussion with the patient and multidisciplinary team involving physicians, surgeons and anaesthetists. ( Quality of evidence: low; Recommendation: strong )

[wbe29d1-130] Hepatic hydrothorax Hepatic hydrothorax (HH) is accumulation of transudative fluid in the pleural space in the absence of cardiac, pulmonary or pleural disease, affecting approximately 5–12% of patients with advanced liver disease. 235 The first-line management of hydrothorax is based on controlling ascites with diuretics and/or LVP as discussed previously. However, pleural effusion can persist despite successful treatment of ascites, defined as refractory hydrothorax. 47

[wbe29d1-131] Therapeutic thoracentesis is required to provide symptomatic relief from dyspnoea but the effect is transient. Repeated procedures increase the risks of complications, including pneumothorax, bleeding and pleural infection. 236 TIPSS has been suggested either as a definitive treatment or as a bridge to transplantation in patients with refractory hydrothorax. Meta-analysis of six retrospective studies with a total of 198 patients reported a response rate of 56%. 237 Mortality within 45 days of TIPSS placement was 18%, mainly related to older age and the severity of liver disease. However, without any data on comparison with standard treatment, it is difficult to interpret the impact of TIPSS on survival. It is also worth noting that only one of these studies reported on patients treated with PTFE-covered stents. 238 In patients with contraindications to TIPSS or liver transplant, a permanent indwelling pleural catheter can be considered in conjunction with the patient and the multidisciplinary team taking into account the infection risk.

[wbe29d1-132] Recommendation TIPSS should be considered in patients with HH after discussion with the multidisciplinary team. ( Quality of evidence: low; Recommendation: strong ) In patients with HH who are not undergoing a TIPSS and/or a liver transplant evaluation, alternative palliative interventions should be considered. ( Quality of evidence: low; Recommendation: strong )

[wbe29d1-133] Non-selective beta-blockers and ascites The portal pressure-lowering effects of non-selective beta-blockers (NSBB) have been known to be beneficial in patients with ascites for three decades. Lebrec’s group demonstrated back in 1991 in a meta-analysis of trial data that NSBB reduce the likelihood of first variceal haemorrhage in the ascites subgroup, while in Child’s Pugh B and C cirrhosis the addition of NSBB to band ligation results in less variceal rebleeding and superior survival. 239 240 Proven haemodynamic response to NSBB (drop in hepatic venous pressure gradient (HVPG) of ≥10–20% from baseline, or to <12 mm Hg) has been linked with a lower probability of the development of ascites; and in patients already with ascites, a lower probability of refractory ascites and hepatorenal syndrome. 241 242 However, it remains possible that non-response in this context is simply a surrogate marker for disease severity.

[wbe29d1-134] In recent years, there has been increasing recognition that the benefits of NSBB in patients with cirrhosis may not be exclusively explained by the reduction in portal pressure. NSBB reduce markers of intestinal permeability, bacterial translocation, systemic inflammation and the incidence of spontaneous bacterial peritonitis independently of haemodynamic response, suggesting a direct effect, potentially via intestinal transit time or on the bowel mucosal integrity. 243–245 Given the mounting evidence that bacterial translocation and the systemic inflammatory response contribute to the downward spiral of circulatory dysfunction in cirrhosis, it follows that NSBB may also reduce non-bleeding related mortality. 61 246

[wbe29d1-135] Despite all this, Lebrec et al

[wbe29d1-136] 194 introduced controversy to the beta-blocker story in 2010 when his team reported that patients with refractory ascites receiving NSBB had greater all-cause mortality during long-term follow-up. 247 The window hypothesis has since been proposed, which postulates that refractory ascites represents the tipping point in portal hypertension when the cardio-inhibitory effects of NSBB compromise organ perfusion. 246 Krag and coauthors suggest that the ‘window’ of benefit from NSBB opens when grade 2 oesophageal varices develop and closes with the development of refractory ascites. Yet, concerns have been raised about the methodology of the original observational study; and many subsequent studies, including five meta-analyses and three with propensity risk score matching, have shown either similar or superior survival in patients with refractory ascites receiving NSBB. 175 240 247–253 Of relevance is that the Lebrec data observed patients receiving a relatively high dose of NSBB compared with many of the other cohorts studied, and the NSBB type (carvedilol vs propranolol) may be important. 247 254

[wbe29d1-137] We conclude that, until randomised high-quality data are available, the current evidence supports the use of NSBB when indicated in patients with refractory ascites ( online supplemental table 11 ), unless alternative markers of circulatory failure, such as hypotension or reduced glomerular filtration rate, are present. 175 Recommendation Refractory ascites should not be viewed as a contraindication to NSBB. ( Quality of evidence: moderate; Recommendation: strong )

[wbe29d1-138] Patients with refractory ascites who are taking NSBB should be monitored closely, and dose reduction or discontinuation may be appropriate in those who develop hypotension or acute/progressive renal dysfunction. ( Quality of evidence: moderate; Recommendation: strong )

[wbe29d1-139] Automated low-flow ascites pump (ALFApump) The ALFApump system consists of a subcutaneously implanted battery-powered programmable pump. It is connected to catheters that transfer ascites from the peritoneal cavity to the bladder, from which it is eliminated with urine. Initially, two multicentre safety and efficacy studies 255 256 reported substantial reduction of the number and volume of paracenteses in patients with advanced cirrhosis and refractory ascites. However, adverse effects directly related to the device occurred in up to 39% of cases. In a multicentre RCT in patients with refractory ascites, the ALFApump significantly reduced the need for paracentesis and was associated with significantly improved chronic liver disease questionnaire, nutritional parameters such as hand-grip strength and body mass index. 257 Meta-analysis of the data from the RCT and several case series showed that 62% of the patients did not require LVP following pump insertion. 258 Pooled estimate rates were 30% for acute kidney injury, 27% for bacterial peritonitis and 20% for urinary tract infection. The device had no effect on survival. Currently, NICE recommends that the ALFApump should be used only with special arrangements for clinical governance, consent and audit or research. 259

[wbe29d1-140] Recommendation Automated low-flow ascites pump should be considered only in special circumstances with robust arrangements of clinical governance, audit or research. ( Quality of evidence: low; Recommendation: weak ) Hepatorenal syndrome Patients with cirrhosis and ascites can develop a specific form of renal dysfunction, which is termed hepatorenal syndrome (HRS). Traditionally HRS was thought to be due to the altered haemodynamic alterations with hyperdynamic circulation as well as overactive endogenous vasoactive system which results in renal hypoperfusion. 260 It is now recognised that systemic inflammation also plays an important role in the pathophysiology of HRS. 261 262

[wbe29d1-141] The traditional classification of HRS-1 and HRS-2 has recently been revised by the International Club of Ascites. 263 HRS-1, which reflects a rapid reduction in renal function, has been proposed to be changed to HRS-acute kidney injury (HRS-AKI). The new definition of HRS-AKI includes an increase in serum creatinine of ≥0.3 mg/dL (27 µmol/L) within 48 hours or ≥50% from baseline, without necessitating a final cut-off value of 1.5 mg/dL (133 µmol/L). This allows treatment to be started earlier. HRS-2, which represents renal dysfunction that does not progress rapidly, has been proposed to be changed to HRS-NAKI (non-AKI). 263

[wbe29d1-142] The mainstay of treatment of HRS-AKI involves HAS and vasoconstrictors, particularly terlipressin. Combination of terlipressin and HAS has been shown in RCTs to significantly improve renal function in HRS-AKI and improve short-term mortality ( online supplemental table 12 ). 264–266 Higher baseline serum creatinine is an independent predictor of failure to respond to vasoconstrictor treatment. 262 267 A full review of HRS is beyond the scope of this guideline.

[wbe29d1-143] Palliative care Palliative care is integral to the management of advanced non-cancer conditions, such as cardiac, respiratory and renal disease, often supported by practice guidelines. 268–270 Patients with cirrhosis and ascites often report a poor quality of life, 271 this being an independent predictor of 12-month mortality. 272 However, only a minority of patients with advanced cirrhosis receive timely palliative care and this has been discussed in recent reviews. 273 274 A British study indicated that from 2013 to 2015, of the 45 000 cirrhosis-related deaths, about a third required LVP in the last year of their life, overall healthcare costs being over £21 000 per person. 275

[wbe29d1-144] Substantial literature supports the use of palliative tunnelled long-term abdominal drains (LTAD) in individuals with refractory ascites due to malignancy, including NICE medical technology guidance. 276–279 These drains are inserted in hospital and allow drainage of small amounts of ascites 2–3 times a week at home. Potential advantages over LVP include symptom-guided drainage and avoidance of repeated hospitalisations. A recent systematic review on LTAD in cirrhosis showed that the majority of patients could be managed in the community. 280

[wbe29d1-145] Results from a recent feasibility RCT comparing palliative LTAD with LVP in refractory ascites due to cirrhosis have just been published (REDUCe study). 281 In this 3-month study, 36 patients were randomised, 19 to LVP and 17 to LTAD. All patients received prophylactic antibiotics for the study duration. Following randomisation, the median number (IQR) of hospital ascitic drains for LTAD versus LVP groups were 0 (0, 1) versus 4 (3, 7), respectively. Only two patients allocated to LTAD required hospital admissions specifically for ascites drainage. Self-limiting cellulitis/leakage occurred in 41% (7/17) in the LTAD vs 11% (2/19) in the LVP group; peritonitis incidence being 6% (1/17) vs 11% (2/19), respectively. Median (IQR) fortnightly community/hospital/social care ascites-related costs were lower in the LTAD group than in the LVP group, £329 (253, 580) versus £843 (603, 1060), respectively. Qualitative data (currently only published as a summary) indicate that LTAD could transform the care pathway. 281

[wbe29d1-146] The REDUCe study demonstrates feasibility, with preliminary evidence of LTAD acceptability, effectiveness and safety and reduction in health resource use. Future trials should assess LTAD as a palliative intervention for refractory ascites in cirrhosis. Recommendations Patients with refractory ascites who are not undergoing evaluation for liver transplant should be offered a palliative care referral. Besides repeated LVP, alternative palliative interventions for refractory ascites should also be considered. ( Quality of evidence: weak; Recommendation: strong )

[wbe29d1-147] Conclusions The development of ascites is a landmark in the natural history of cirrhosis. Therefore, it should be considered an important time point at which an individual patient’s suitability for liver transplantation which is a definitive treatment of ascites and its complications, should be determined. Over the years, there has been a substantial improvement in care of patients with cirrhosis, including those with ascites. A study involving over 780 000 hospitalisations of patients with cirrhosis demonstrated an improvement in inpatient survival over a decade despite higher age and more medically complex disease. 282 This was remarkably consistent across several cirrhosis complications, suggesting improved cirrhosis care beyond general improvements in inpatient care. Future research should focus on areas of need and questions where there is no high-quality evidence to guide the management of ascites.

[wbe29d1-148] Research recommendations Randomised controlled trials (RCTs) with large sample size should evaluate the role of antibiotics in the secondary prophylaxis for SBP in ascites secondary to cirrhosis. Large RCTs should assess the role of midodrine in the management of ascites. Cost-effectiveness of long-term administration of HAS to patients with decompensated cirrhosis and ascites should be evaluated. Role of nutritional interventions in the management of ascites should be evaluated. Large RCT of long-term carvedilol versus no carvedilol in patients with refractory ascites without large oesophageal varices should be carried out.

[wbe29d1-149] Role of TIPSS in the management of hepatic hydrothorax should be compared with other therapeutic interventions. The cost-effectiveness and the effect of automated low-flow ascites pumps on the quality of life of patients with refractory ascites should be evaluated. Effectiveness and safety of long-term abdominal drains should be assessed in RCTs for the palliative care of patients with cirrhosis and refractory ascites.

[wbe29d1-150] We are grateful to Shani Steer, voluntary peer mentor, Brighton for her involvement and review of these guidelines. We wish to thank the BSG Liver Section for support and internal review of the guideline. We are grateful to BASL for review and endorsement of this guideline. We also thank Peuish Sugathan and Nottingham University Hospitals NHS Trust for providing data on the underlying aetiology in patients with new onset ascites.

[wc2ce73-4] 17 10 2026 10 2026 14 8 522623 10.1002/ueg2.v14.8 e70317 03 10 2026 22 5 2026 10 10 2026 17 10 2026 18 10 2026 18 10 2026 © 2026 The Author(s). United European Gastroenterology Journal published by Wiley Periodicals LLC on behalf of United European Gastroenterology. https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/ This is an open access article under the terms of the http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/ License, which permits use and distribution in any medium, provided the original work is properly cited, the use is non‐commercial and no modifications or adaptations are made. ABSTRACT Bacterial infections are a major cause of mortality in cirrhosis, with antibiotic prophylaxis and empirical treatment embedded in international guidelines. However, the prevalence of multidrug‐resistant organisms and the epidemiology of infections have shifted considerably in recent decades. This review critically appraises the evidence underlying guideline recommendations on antibiotic prophylaxis for spontaneous bacterial peritonitis and acute variceal bleeding in cirrhosis, and antimicrobial management in acute liver failure. The evidence‐underlying current recommendations is limited and predates the current era of widespread antimicrobial resistance, challenging their applicability in current practice. Emerging evidence suggests that primary prophylaxis should be reconsidered, alternatives such as rifaximin warrant further evaluation against established fluoroquinolone regimens, and shorter antibiotic courses may be non‐inferior to standard durations. New double‐blind placebo‐controlled, non‐inferiority and Bayesian trials are needed to evaluate antibiotic regimens and microbiome‐ and barrier‐directed interventions, supported by improved diagnostics, while minimising treatment duration, toxicity and selection for antimicrobial resistance. acute liver failure antibiotic prophylaxis antimicrobial resistance antimicrobial stewardship cirrhosis spontaneous bacterial peritonitis variceal bleeding pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access yes pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript no pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement yes pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes pmc-license-ref CC BY-NC-ND source-schema-version-number 2.0 cover-date October 2026 details-of-publishers-convertor Converter:WILEY_ML3GV2_TO_JATSPMC version:6.8.8 mode:remove_FC converted:17.10.2026 1 Introduction Antibiotics are central to preventing and managing complications in cirrhosis, given the high incidence of bacterial infections and associated mortality [ 1 , 2 ]. Susceptibility is driven by cirrhosis‐associated immune dysfunction, increased bacterial translocation and gut microbiome alterations [ 3 , 4 , 5 ]. In Europe, a 2025 meta‐analysis estimated that bacterial infections affect 38% of patients with cirrhosis, with a rising global trend over recent decades [ 6 ]. This trend is likely driven by improved chronic liver disease management leading to prolonged healthcare exposure, and increased use of invasive procedures [ 6 , 7 , 8 ].

[wc2ce73-5] In parallel, the epidemiology of infections has shifted, with an increasing prevalence of multidrug‐resistant organisms (MDROs) and changing infection sites, as infections other than spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) now account for most bacterial infections in cirrhosis [ 9 , 10 ]. These include urinary tract infections (24.6%), respiratory tract infections (20%), bacteraemia (10.5%), and skin and soft‐tissue infections (7.2%) [ 10 ]. Causative organisms vary by infection site, with Enterococcus spp. commonly reported in urinary tract infections and Streptococcus spp. commonly reported in respiratory tract infections. Overall, Gram‐negative bacteria remain predominant, particularly Escherichia coli, whereas Gram‐positive organisms such as Enterococcus spp. are increasingly prevalent.

[wc2ce73-6] MDROs are defined as microorganisms with acquired non‐susceptibility to at least one agent in three or more antimicrobial categories [ 11 ]. Examples include Gram‐positive organisms such as methicillin‐resistant Staphylococcus aureus and vancomycin‐resistant Enterococcus faecium, and Gram‐negative organisms such as third‐generation cephalosporin‐resistant E . coli and carbapenem‐resistant Pseudomonas aeruginosa [ 12 ]. Worldwide, MDROs accounted for 34% of culture‐positive infections in patients with cirrhosis between 2015 and 2016, and were associated with increased mortality [ 9 ]. Repeated and prolonged antibiotic use drives MDRO emergence, underscoring the need for antimicrobial stewardship and reassessment of current prophylaxis and treatment strategies [ 13 ].

[wc2ce73-7] International guidelines consistently recommend antibiotic prophylaxis for primary and secondary prevention of SBP and acute variceal bleeding (AVB), although the latter represents pre‐emptive therapy [ 14 , 15 ]. However, many recommendations are based on trials performed decades ago, before widespread antimicrobial resistance (AMR) and changing infection epidemiology, raising questions about their current applicability. Similar concerns apply to empirical antibiotic management in acute liver failure (ALF) [ 16 ]. Moreover, MDRO colonisation following antibiotic exposure is associated with increased mortality in liver transplant candidates and may complicate transplantation outcomes [ 17 ].

[wc2ce73-8] This review critically appraises the evidence underlying antibiotic prophylaxis in SBP and AVB in cirrhosis, examining whether the recent data support or challenge current recommendations. We also reassess the evidence for empirical antibiotic management in ALF.

[wc2ce73-9] 2 Methods Evidence underlying current guideline recommendations was identified from the latest international and national clinical practice guidelines, including the European Association for the Study of the Liver (EASL), American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases (AASLD), Baveno VI and VII consensus, British Society of Gastroenterology (BSG), National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE), Asian Pacific Association for the Study of the Liver (APASL) and American College of Gastroenterology (ACG).

[wc2ce73-10] A targeted PubMed search identified articles up to April 2026, addressing antibiotic prophylaxis in SBP and AVB in cirrhosis, and empirical antibiotic management in ALF. Search terms included combinations of ‘cirrhosis’, ‘spontaneous bacterial peritonitis’, ‘variceal bleeding’, ‘acute liver failure’, ‘antibiotic’ and ‘prophylaxis’ alongside relevant MeSH terms. Priority was given to randomised controlled trials, meta‐analyses and large cohort studies. The search focussed on recent evidence that may support or challenge current strategies.

[wc2ce73-11] 3 Results 3.1 Primary SBP Prophylaxis Guidelines vary in recommendation strength regarding primary prophylaxis, reflecting uncertainty and limitations in the underlying evidence (Table 1 ) [ 14 , 15 , 21 , 24 , 26 , 28 ]. Most guidelines recommend primary prophylaxis only for selected high‐risk patients, generally defined by low ascitic fluid protein (< 15 g/L [1.5 g/dL]) combined with advanced liver disease, renal dysfunction, or hyponatraemia (Supporting Information S1 : Table S1) [ 14 , 15 , 26 ]. EASL and APASL recommend norfloxacin, while AASLD recommends ciprofloxacin or co‐trimoxazole, as norfloxacin is unavailable in the United States [ 14 , 15 , 26 ]. BSG recommends prophylaxis based on low ascitic fluid protein alone, without specifying a particular antibiotic regimen [ 24 ]. NICE takes the most restrictive approach, advising against routine prophylaxis while allowing consideration in severe liver disease, with antibiotic choice guided by local microbiological advice [ 28 ]. This approach reflects limited norfloxacin availability in the United Kingdom and fluoroquinolone restrictions related to adverse effects, AMR, and Clostridioides difficile infection [ 28 , 31 , 32 ]. Duration is rarely addressed, with only EASL and NICE explicitly recommending continuing prophylaxis until ascites resolves [ 14 , 28 ].

[wc2ce73-12] TABLE 1 Visual overview of guideline recommendations for antibiotic prophylaxis and empirical treatment. [wc2ce73-13] Continue until ascites resolves Secondary SBP prophylaxis Prophylaxis after previous SBP Only considered in severe liver disease Ciprofloxacin or co‐trimoxazole listed as alternatives Follow local microbiological advice Long‐term prophylaxis recommended Discontinuation upon recompensation AVB Antibiotic prophylaxis recommended Ceftriaxone preferred or recommended Shorter course considered Based on infection risk and cirrhosis severity ALF Microbiological surveillance recommended Empirical antibiotics when infection is suspected or upon clinical deterioration Routine antibiotic prophylaxis recommended

[wc2ce73-14] Note: Green indicates a recommendation by the guideline, orange indicates a conditional recommendation, red indicates a recommendation against, and grey indicates that the topic is not addressed. Dark shades indicate a strong recommendation, whereas lighter shades indicate a weak, ungraded* or conditional** recommendation. Abbreviations: AASLD = American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases; ALF = Acute Liver Failure; APASL = Asian Pacific Association for the Study of the Liver; AVB = Acute Variceal Bleeding; BSG = British Society of Gastroenterology; EASL = European Association for the Study of the Liver; NICE = National Institute for Health and Care Excellence.

[wc2ce73-15] The key trial underlying primary prophylaxis recommendations is Fernandez et al. a placebo‐controlled trial with norfloxacin whose inclusion criteria largely shaped current prophylaxis criteria [ 33 ]. Among 68 patients, norfloxacin improved 3‐month survival (94% vs. 62%, p = 0.003), whereas the difference in 1‐year survival was borderline significant (60% vs. 48%, p = 0.05). However, mortality findings were inconsistent across the two meta‐analyses [ 34 , 35 ]. Saab et al. found no significant effect (risk ratio [RR] 0.72, 95% confidence interval [CI] 0.48–1.06, p = 0.09), whereas Loomba et al. reported reduced mortality (odds ratio [OR] 0.60, 95% CI 0.37–0.97, p = 0.034), despite including the same four randomised trials. This discrepancy was attributed to Saab et al.’s use of additional unpublished data [ 36 , 37 ]. Despite these inconsistencies, EASL provides a strong recommendation for primary prophylaxis in selected high‐risk patients [ 14 ].

[wc2ce73-16] AASLD also considered the NORFLOCIR trial by Moreau et al. (2018, n = 291), which compared norfloxacin with placebo in advanced cirrhosis, although < 5% had prior SBP [ 38 ]. This larger but underpowered trial did not confirm a survival benefit, with benefit suggested only in a post hoc subgroup with low ascitic fluid protein. A subsequent meta‐analysis by Facciorusso et al. suggested lower mortality with norfloxacin, although the effect remained borderline significant (RR 0.68, 95% CI 0.47–0.99, p = 0.04) [ 39 ]. Reflecting this uncertainty, AASLD states that primary prophylaxis may be considered in selected high‐risk patients [ 15 ]. APASL similarly recommends primary prophylaxis but grades the evidence as low based on the Cochrane meta‐analysis by Komolafe et al. [ 26 , 40 ]. This meta‐analysis found no significant mortality benefit for any antibiotic regimen and graded the evidence as very low, without differences between primary and secondary prophylaxis.

[wc2ce73-17] Whether current guideline recommendations remain applicable is uncertain. Norfloxacin efficacy in preventing SBP has declined substantially over the past 3 decades, likely driven by rising MDROs [ 41 , 42 ]. This prompted interest in alternatives to fluoroquinolones, including rifaximin and co‐trimoxazole [ 43 , 44 ]. Rifaximin may reduce MDRO selection, given its local action in the gut with minimal systemic absorption [ 45 ]. It inhibits bacterial RNA polymerase, has broad‐spectrum activity against Gram‐positive and Gram‐negative bacteria, and modulates the gut microbiome [ 45 , 46 ]. Established indications include traveller's diarrhoea and hepatic encephalopathy, with off‐label use for small intestinal bacterial overgrowth, and adverse events generally limited to mild nausea and respiratory or urinary tract infections [ 46 , 47 ]. However, rifaximin may be selected for RNA polymerase mutations that mediate rifampicin resistance and cross‐resistance to daptomycin in vancomycin‐resistant E. faecium [ 48 ]. Although these mutations are not horizontally transferable, resistant strains may be selected rapidly and spread through clonal expansion [ 46 , 48 ]. A large multinational cohort also found an almost two‐fold increased risk of AMR and multidrug resistance associated with rifaximin use [ 49 ].

[wc2ce73-18] Evidence for rifaximin in primary prophylaxis is mixed (Table 2 ) [ 43 , 50 , 51 ]. Assem et al. found alternating norfloxacin and rifaximin superior to norfloxacin alone, whereas Praharaj et al. found rifaximin and norfloxacin to be comparably effective in primary prophylaxis [ 43 , 50 ]. However, Thévenot et al. found no 12‐month survival benefit with rifaximin over placebo or reduction in SBP or other liver‐related complications, although post hoc analysis suggested a survival benefit [ 51 ]. Similarly, the ASEPTIC trial (2025) found no benefit of co‐trimoxazole over placebo for survival or hospitalisation in 442 patients, even among patients with low ascitic fluid protein, leading the authors to argue against routine primary prophylaxis given AMR concerns [ 52 , 58 ].

[wc2ce73-23] Overall, evidence supporting primary prophylaxis appears less robust than current guidelines suggest and predates the current era of widespread AMR. With ASEPTIC providing the first adequately powered trial in this setting, its negative findings warrant reconsideration of primary prophylaxis.

[wc2ce73-24] 3.2 Secondary SBP Prophylaxis Guidelines broadly recommend secondary prophylaxis after a prior SBP episode [ 14 , 15 , 21 , 24 , 26 ]. Norfloxacin is the standard regimen, with ciprofloxacin or co‐trimoxazole as alternatives if norfloxacin is unavailable (Table 1 ). AASLD and APASL recommend long‐term prophylaxis without specifying duration, whereas Baveno VIII recommends discontinuation upon recompensation [ 15 , 22 , 26 ].

[wc2ce73-25] Secondary prophylaxis is primarily supported by the 1990 trial by Ginés et al., which randomised 80 patients with prior SBP to norfloxacin or placebo and reduced 1‐year SBP recurrence from 68% to 20% ( p = 0.0063) [ 59 ]. The superiority of norfloxacin over placebo was supported by the meta‐analysis by Mücke et al. which pooled predominantly older trials, although no clear superiority over alternative antibiotics was shown [ 42 ]. Ciprofloxacin and co‐trimoxazole are supported as alternatives by low‐quality evidence [ 24 , 26 ].

[wc2ce73-26] Rifaximin appeared comparable with other antibiotics and in some meta‐analyses even more favourable, yet most guidelines await for further trials before recommending it [ 60 , 61 ]. Praharaj et al. reported lower SBP recurrence with rifaximin than norfloxacin in the secondary prophylaxis subgroup (7.7% vs. 39.4%, p = 0.004) but no significant difference in mortality [ 43 ]. However, this subgroup analysis was limited by the small sample size and the trial was underpowered for mortality. While not directly addressing SBP prophylaxis, the LIVERHOPE trial (2025) found no reduction in the incidence of bacterial infections with rifaximin plus simvastatin compared with placebo in patients with decompensated cirrhosis (23.9% vs. 25.8%), although infection was a secondary outcome for which the trial was also not powered [ 62 ]. Further high‐quality trials are needed before rifaximin can be considered a valid alternative, with several registered trials currently evaluating its efficacy (Table 2 ) [ 53 , 54 ].

[wc2ce73-27] More recent evidence on fluoroquinolone prophylaxis comes largely from cohort studies. Shetty et al. reported low SBP recurrence at 1 year, whereas Silvey et al. observed higher recurrence rates likely driven by fluoroquinolone resistance [ 63 , 64 ]. Although studied in primary prophylaxis, Badal et al. (2023) reported fluoroquinolone resistance in 34% of patients receiving ciprofloxacin prophylaxis versus 14% in those without, with comparable findings for co‐trimoxazole (40% vs. 14%) [ 13 ]. Similarly, Mücke et al. reported secondary infections in 42.9% of patients during quinolone‐based prophylaxis in a mixed primary and secondary cohort, including SBP in 16.9% [ 41 ]. Quinolone‐resistant Gram‐negative bacteria were independently associated with prophylaxis failure. Regular MDRO screening has therefore been proposed to guide treatment and to re‐evaluate prophylaxis although evidence for alternative regimens remains limited.

[wc2ce73-28] While secondary prophylaxis is widely recommended as the standard of care, the evidence rests on a single placebo‐controlled trial from more than 30 years ago, with subsequent studies comparing different antibiotics rather than placebos. Uncertainties remain regarding optimal antibiotic choice, efficacy during widespread AMR, and prophylaxis duration.

[wc2ce73-29] 3.3 AVB Prophylaxis AVB mortality declined from 42.6% in 1980 to 14.5% by the 2000s, with studies in the 1990s showing improved survival with antibiotic prophylaxis by reducing infections and early rebleeding [ 65 , 66 , 67 , 68 ]. Accordingly, guidelines uniformly recommend antibiotic prophylaxis (Table 1 ). Intravenous ceftriaxone remains the preferred agent due to its gram‐negative coverage, parenteral administration in critically ill patients, and increasing quinolone resistance [ 69 ]. However, recent European surveillance data show a 5.9% increase in third‐generation cephalosporin‐resistant E. coli bloodstream infections between 2019 and 2024, raising concerns about the long‐term efficacy of this strategy [ 12 ].

[wc2ce73-30] Baveno VIII recommends antibiotic prophylaxis in all patients presenting with AVB, while suggesting that duration and need may be individualised based on infection risk and cirrhosis severity [ 22 ]. Recommendations on optimal treatment duration have shifted from up to 7 days in older guidelines to shorter courses in recent AASLD and APASL guidelines [ 19 , 27 ]. Since Baveno VI and the review by Fernandez et al. experts have called for trials omitting antibiotics in Child–Pugh A patients [ 22 , 23 , 70 ]. Crocombe proposed that patients with Child–Pugh score < 7 and successful endoscopic therapy may not require antibiotics, while shorter courses may suffice in higher‐risk patients without rebleeding [ 71 ]. Meta‐analyses by Prosty et al. and Bot et al. question the presumed mortality benefit of prolonged prophylaxis, even in patients with advanced liver disease (Child‐Pugh class B and C), with studies published after 2015 favouring shorter (2–3 days) or on‐demand strategies [ 55 , 72 , 73 ]. Supporting evidence includes a non‐inferiority study by Lo et al. a randomised controlled trial by Gupta et al. and several observational studies (Table 2 ) [ 56 , 57 , 74 , 75 , 76 ]. Together, these findings support a shift towards personalised, shorter antibiotic strategies, rendering older guideline recommendations on duration less applicable.

[wc2ce73-31] 3.4 Management of ALF Early recognition and treatment of infection are essential in ALF management, whereas antibiotic prophylaxis remains debated. All three guidelines recommend microbiological surveillance, including periodic cultures of sputum, urine and blood for bacterial and fungal organisms, although the optimal frequency remains unknown (Table 1 ) [ 18 , 20 , 30 ]. Recognition of bacterial infection in ALF is challenging, as clinical signs are frequently absent and C‐reactive protein and procalcitonin are often unhelpful [ 18 , 20 ]. Hepatic encephalopathy at admission and a systemic inflammatory response syndrome score > 2 are predictors of bacteraemia [ 18 ]. Unexplained fever, leucocytosis, or deterioration in mental status should prompt investigation for infection. None of the guidelines provide a fixed antibiotic regimen or support routine prophylactic antibiotics in ALF.

[wc2ce73-32] Guidelines on empiric antibiotic treatment refer, directly or indirectly, to the prospective cohort study by Rolando et al. from the 1990s [ 77 , 78 ]. This study evaluated 50 hospitalised patients with ALF, of whom 90% had microbiologically proven or clinical evidence of infection [ 77 ]. Despite antibiotic treatment, 30 patients died with proven infection documented in most cases. Although all guidelines acknowledge the limited and outdated evidence base, EASL strongly recommends empirical antibiotic therapy [ 18 ].

[wc2ce73-33] For antibiotic prophylaxis, EASL and AASLD refer to the 1993 trial by Rolando et al., with ACG referring to it indirectly [ 18 , 30 , 78 , 79 ]. This trial randomised 104 patients with fulminant liver failure to a selective parenteral and enteral antimicrobial regimen (SPEAR), cefuroxime alone, or no initial antimicrobials [ 79 ]. Selective intestinal decontamination differs from systemic antibiotic prophylaxis, as it uses poorly absorbable oral antibiotics to reduce intestinal colonisation rather than systemic antibiotic exposure to prevent infection. SPEAR reduced infections compared with no prophylaxis (34% vs. 61%, p < 0.005) but offered no benefit over cefuroxime alone, suggesting this effect was driven by early systemic therapy rather than enteral decontamination. Be that as it may, overall survival did not differ between the groups. A retrospective study by Salmerón et al. found that selective intestinal decontamination reduced infections caused by enterobacteria, without improving survival [ 80 ]. EASL included a more recent retrospective cohort by Karvellas et al. of 1551 patients with ALF, in which antimicrobial prophylaxis did not reduce bloodstream infection (12.8% vs. 15.7%, p = 0.12) nor improve 21‐day survival (70% vs. 70%, p = 0.88) [ 81 ]. Given that the evidence is limited to a single trial and retrospective cohort data, all guidelines rated its quality as low.

[wc2ce73-34] Overall, evidence underlying ALF recommendations is limited, with recent data arguing against routine prophylaxis. In a related high‐risk population, a randomised controlled trial in patients with severe alcohol‐related hepatitis treated with prednisolone found that amoxicillin‐clavulanate did not improve 60‐day survival compared with placebo, despite reducing infections [ 82 ]. Moreover, multidrug‐resistant bacteria were more frequent in the antibiotic group. Similarly, current evidence is insufficient to recommend routine selective intestinal decontamination or systemic antibiotic prophylaxis in transplant candidates who may subsequently undergo immunosuppressive therapy [ 18 , 20 , 30 ]. Given the rarity and heterogeneity of ALF, and the risk of drug‐induced liver injury in severe liver dysfunction, high‐quality evidence supporting empirical antibiotic therapy remains lacking [ 83 ].

[wc2ce73-35] 3.5 Future Perspectives The evidence underlying current recommendations in cirrhosis is limited by methodological heterogeneity and small sample sizes across many studies, undermining the reliability of several meta‐analyses in this field. Many recommendations also rely on trials conducted 20–30 years ago, while rising MDRO prevalence challenges their effectiveness in current practice (Figure 1 ). This supports new double‐blind, placebo‐controlled randomised trials in contemporary cohorts to reassess established prophylactic strategies, such as norfloxacin‐based SBP prophylaxis, in the era of widespread AMR. Non‐inferiority trials may also be valuable, although they often require large sample sizes [ 84 , 85 ]. A proposed approach uses the observed difference in efficacy rather than its confidence interval to reduce sample size, combined with predefined pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic parameters and quality of life [ 85 ]. Bayesian methods may further improve feasibility by incorporating previous trial data, to reduce sample size [ 86 ]. Future trials should also longitudinally assess the gut microbiome and resistome to determine how prophylactic regimens affect microbial composition and select for AMR genes [ 87 , 88 ].

[wc2ce73-36] FIGURE 1 Future perspectives for antibiotic prophylaxis and treatment in cirrhosis.

[wc2ce73-37] Beyond improved trial design, antimicrobial stewardship is essential to limit the rise of MDROs. Wider clinical adoption of rapid molecular diagnostics such as multiplex polymerase chain reaction syndromic tests, or whole‐genome sequencing, could identify pathogens and resistance genes simultaneously and enable more targeted antibiotic treatment [ 89 , 90 ]. In ALF specifically, more reliable biomarkers are needed to distinguish bacterial infection from sterile inflammation [ 18 , 30 ]. Presepsin, a soluble CD14 fragment released during innate immune activation, is a promising biomarker [ 91 ]. Higher presepsin concentrations have been reported in patients with sepsis compared with non‐infectious systemic inflammation [ 92 ]. Further research should determine its diagnostic value and optimal thresholds in ALF.

[wc2ce73-38] Improving vaccination coverage is another key strategy to reduce bacterial infections and antibiotic use in at‐risk populations, with pneumococcal vaccination rates reaching only 7.4% within the first year of chronic liver disease diagnosis [ 93 ]. The risk of invasive pneumococcal disease is 2–13‐fold higher in patients with chronic liver disease than in the general population [ 94 ]. Moreover, S. pneumoniae is a common cause of community‐acquired pneumonia and SBP in cirrhosis, supporting pneumococcal vaccination in this population [ 94 , 95 ]. Beyond vaccination, shortening antibiotic treatment is another effective and low‐cost intervention, especially when improved trial designs as mentioned above are used. Collectively, these measures can strengthen antimicrobial stewardship in cirrhosis.

[wc2ce73-39] Even with optimal stewardship, alternatives like rifaximin may unintentionally drive cross‐resistance to daptomycin, underscoring the need for non‐antibiotic strategies [ 48 ]. Modulation of the gut microbiome and intestinal barrier may reduce AMR, bacterial translocation and infection risk. Novel strategies include faecal microbiota transplantation to reduce gut carriage of resistance genes, bacteriophage therapy to selectively target pathogenic strains, and microRNA‐based therapies to strengthen barrier function, potentially reducing infection burden and antibiotic use [ 96 , 97 ].

[wc2ce73-40] 4 Conclusion Recommendations on antibiotic prophylaxis in cirrhosis and empirical treatment in acute liver failure rest on a limited evidence base that predates the current era of widespread antibiotic resistance, questioning their applicability in current practice. Emerging evidence suggests that primary prophylaxis in high‐risk patients should be reconsidered, alternative agents such as rifaximin warrant further evaluation against fluoroquinolone regimens, and shorter courses may be non‐inferior to standard durations. Robust evaluation will require new double‐blind, placebo‐controlled trials as well as non‐inferiority and Bayesian trial designs, supported by improved diagnostics, to evaluate antibiotic regimens and microbiome‐directed interventions that remain effective while minimising treatment duration, toxicity, and antibiotic resistance.
