This article is an open access article distributed under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY) license . Background : Fluoroquinolone secondary prophylaxis of spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) was established in a fluoroquinolone-naïve era. We re-appraised the randomized evidence comparing rifaximin with norfloxacin and its robustness to missing outcome data and competing mortality. Methods : We searched MEDLINE, Embase, CENTRAL, and Scopus through 15 July 2026. The primary estimand was the intention-to-treat risk ratio (RR) for six-month SBP recurrence, with death as a competing event. Trials were pooled with REML random-effects models and Hartung–Knapp–Sidik–Jonkman (HKSJ) intervals; missing-outcome bounding across all trials and a composite of recurrence or death were post hoc. Certainty was rated with GRADE. The review was registered retrospectively in PROSPERO (CRD420261501120). Results : In three randomized trials (398 randomized patients; none double-blind), rifaximin was associated with fewer recurrences than norfloxacin (RR 0.21, 95% HKSJ CI 0.05–0.89), but the modified HKSJ interval crossed unity (0.03–1.31). Across plausible missing-outcome scenarios, the Mantel–Haenszel RR ranged from 0.15 to 0.73 (worst case 95% CI 0.45–1.17) and for recurrence or death from 0.35 to 0.75 (0.55–1.03). Certainty was very low. Conclusions : Rifaximin may reduce SBP recurrence compared with norfloxacin, but the evidence is very uncertain and sensitive to unreported outcomes; a contemporary, blinded trial is needed before guideline change. spontaneous bacterial peritonitis cirrhosis rifaximin norfloxacin secondary prophylaxis antimicrobial resistance meta-analysis This research received no external funding. pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access yes pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript no pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement yes pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes pmc-license-ref CC BY 1. Introduction Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) is a severe, potentially life-threatening infection of ascitic fluid that arises without a surgically treatable intra-abdominal source and represents one of the defining complications of decompensated cirrhosis [ 1 ]. Even after a first episode is successfully treated, the cumulative probability of recurrence within one year approaches 70% [ 2 ], and each recurrence carries substantial mortality and accelerates the trajectory toward acute-on-chronic liver failure [ 3 , 4 ]. On the strength of this recurrence risk, international guidelines—including those of the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases (AASLD) and the European Association for the Study of the Liver (EASL)—have for three decades recommended indefinite oral fluoroquinolone secondary prophylaxis, most commonly norfloxacin or ciprofloxacin, as standard care [ 5 , 6 ]. That recommendation rests largely on a small number of trials conducted in the late twentieth century, when fluoroquinolone-naïve gut flora made these agents both highly active and durably protective.

[w1ab5f0-2] The microbiological premise underlying those trials has since weakened. Decades of population-wide fluoroquinolone exposure have driven a marked rise in infections caused by multidrug-resistant (MDR) organisms, extended-spectrum β-lactamase-producing Enterobacterales, and fluoroquinolone-resistant Gram-negative bacteria [ 3 , 4 ]. In cirrhosis specifically, this shift compromises the regimens intended to prevent SBP, raises the likelihood that empirical therapy for breakthrough infection will fail, and is associated with higher mortality [ 7 ]. Whether long-term fluoroquinolone prophylaxis remains net-beneficial in the contemporary resistance landscape has therefore become an open question [ 8 ].

[w1ab5f0-3] Rifaximin, a poorly absorbed, gut-selective rifamycin used chiefly for hepatic encephalopathy, achieves high intraluminal concentrations with negligible systemic exposure [ 9 , 10 ] and has been reported to exert eubiotic effects on the gut microbiome and intestinal barrier [ 11 ]. These properties have made it a candidate alternative to systemic fluoroquinolones, and it has been examined in several head-to-head trials.

[w1ab5f0-4] The comparative evidence has already been synthesized in part. A Cochrane network meta-analysis of 29 trials, a Bayesian network meta-analysis by Soni et al., and a pairwise review by Song et al. all included rifaximin–norfloxacin comparisons, and the latter two reported estimates for secondary prophylaxis [ 12 , 13 , 14 ]; an earlier pairwise review addressed rifaximin for SBP prophylaxis generally [ 15 ]. These reviews, however, pooled available-case data without examining the analysis populations of the contributing trials, did not incorporate the large observational studies of secondary prophylaxis published in 2025–2026, and, in the case of the network meta-analyses, rated the secondary-prophylaxis evidence as of low or very low certainty without specifying the sources of that uncertainty.

[w1ab5f0-5] The present review therefore does not claim to be the first synthesis of this comparison. Its purpose is a focused re-appraisal: to pool the randomized head-to-head evidence for secondary prophylaxis with small-sample methods appropriate to three trials, to test the robustness of that estimate to the missing outcome data that we identified in the largest trial, and to place the result alongside contemporary cohort evidence on the declining effectiveness of fluoroquinolone-based secondary prophylaxis.

[w1ab5f0-6] 2. Methods 2.1. Design, Reporting, and Registration This systematic review and meta-analysis is reported according to PRISMA 2020 [ 16 ] (completed checklist in Supplementary Table S1 ). The review was registered retrospectively in PROSPERO (CRD420261501120) on 8 September 2026, after the literature search (15 July 2026) and after completion of study selection, data extraction, and the initial analyses (1 September 2026); registration therefore does not document pre-specification of any analysis. Quantitative pooling was restricted to the rifaximin–norfloxacin comparison because it was the only drug pair evaluated in more than one randomized comparison; this decision was made on 7 August 2026, after study selection, and is data-informed. The modified HKSJ interval, the missing-outcome scenarios and the generalized linear model were specified after data extraction; the intention-to-treat estimand with death as a competing event, the extension of the missing-outcome scenarios to all trials, the composite outcome of recurrence or death, and the log-link generalized linear model were adopted during peer review. All of these analyses are labeled post hoc throughout.

[w1ab5f0-7] 2.2. Search Strategy We searched MEDLINE (via PubMed), Embase, the Cochrane Central Register of Controlled Trials (CENTRAL), and Scopus from inception through 15 July 2026. The database search strategies and hit counts are reported in Supplementary Table S2 . No language or date limits were applied. Reference lists of included studies and prior systematic reviews [ 12 , 13 , 14 , 15 ] were screened by hand. Full-text exclusions and reasons are reported in Supplementary Table S3 .

[w1ab5f0-8] 2.3. Eligibility Criteria We included (1) adults with cirrhosis and at least one prior episode of SBP; (2) head-to-head comparisons of antibiotic regimens for secondary SBP prophylaxis or comparisons of prophylaxis with no prophylaxis or placebo; (3) reporting SBP recurrence, all-cause mortality, or resistant infection; and (4) randomized controlled trials (RCTs) or cohort studies. Studies of primary prophylaxis only, studies in which secondary-prophylaxis outcomes could not be separated, and non-comparative reports were excluded.

[w1ab5f0-9] 2.4. Data Extraction and Analysis Populations Two reviewers (J.-H.H. and M.-L.C.) independently screened records, extracted data using a standardized form, and assessed risk of bias; disagreements were resolved by discussion, with adjudication by a third reviewer (N.-J.L.) when needed. Extracted items are listed in Supplementary Table S4 . Event counts, denominators, and deaths were taken from the full texts, including the participant-flow diagrams. Trial investigators were not contacted for unreported data.

[w1ab5f0-10] For Elfert et al. [ 17 ], SBP recurrence is reported for patients completing six months of treatment (rifaximin 4/103; norfloxacin 13/92), although 131 patients were randomized to each arm and the statistical section describes an intention-to-treat analysis; mortality is reported for the randomized population (18/131 vs. 32/131). According to the participant-flow diagram, the 28 rifaximin and 39 norfloxacin non-completers comprised 18 and 32 deaths, 6 and 4 patients lost to follow-up, and 4 and 3 consent withdrawals; recurrence status is therefore unknown for 10 rifaximin and 7 norfloxacin surviving non-completers, and whether any patient who died had recurred before death is not reported. Praharaj et al. [ 18 ] randomized 33 patients to each arm of the pre-specified secondary-prophylaxis stratum and analyzed 26 rifaximin and 33 norfloxacin patients per protocol; the seven excluded rifaximin patients (non-compliance or loss to follow-up) have unknown outcomes, whereas deaths among analyzed patients (7/26 vs. 12/33) are reported in the text. Mostafa et al. [ 19 ] randomized unequally (40:30) and withdrew the five norfloxacin patients who developed recurrence (the trial endpoint), so recurrence status is known for all 70 randomized patients; no deaths were reported.

[w1ab5f0-11] 2.5. Estimand and Outcomes The primary estimand was the intention-to-treat risk ratio (RR) for SBP recurrence within six months, rifaximin versus norfloxacin, among all randomized patients with cirrhosis and prior SBP, with death before recurrence treated as a competing event: patients who died without documented recurrence were counted as recurrence-free, as in a cumulative-incidence estimand. Randomized patients with unknown outcomes for reasons other than death ( Section 2.4 ) were assumed recurrence-free in the primary analysis, and this assumption was varied in sensitivity analyses. The analysis populations reported by the trials (324 of 398 randomized patients) were analyzed as a sensitivity analysis. Because treating death as a competing event can make the arm with higher mortality appear more favorable, we also analyzed the composite of SBP recurrence or death. Secondary outcomes were all-cause mortality and infection with fluoroquinolone-resistant or MDR organisms.

[w1ab5f0-12] 2.6. Risk of Bias and Certainty RCTs were assessed with RoB 2 [ 20 ] and cohort studies with ROBINS-I [ 21 ]. Certainty of evidence for the primary comparison was rated with GRADE [ 22 ], starting at high for randomized evidence and downgrading for risk of bias, inconsistency, indirectness, imprecision, and publication bias according to GRADE guidance. 2.7. Statistical Analysis Quantitative pooling was restricted to the rifaximin–norfloxacin comparison, the only drug pair evaluated in more than one randomized comparison ( Section 2.1 ); other comparisons were synthesized narratively.

[w1ab5f0-13] The primary analysis was a random-effects model with the restricted maximum-likelihood (REML) estimator of τ 2 and HKSJ confidence intervals, chosen because the number of trials was three [ 23 ]. Because the HKSJ interval can be narrower than the Wald interval when observed heterogeneity is below expectation, we also report the modified HKSJ interval of Röver et al., which uses the larger of the HKSJ and Wald standard errors [ 24 ]. For the trial with zero events in one arm (Mostafa et al. [ 19 ], allocated 40:30), 0.5 was added to each cell of that trial only for the inverse-variance analysis. Because a constant correction of 0.5 can bias the estimate when group sizes are unequal [ 25 ], we regard the Mantel–Haenszel (MH) fixed-effect RR, computed without continuity correction, as the preferred estimate for that trial’s contribution. A generalized linear model with fixed study effects and a log link (binomial likelihood, log-risk scale, profile-likelihood confidence interval) was also fitted without continuity correction so that all model-based estimates are on the RR scale. Heterogeneity was quantified with Cochran’s Q and I 2 , recognizing that with three trials I 2 is imprecise and I 2 = 0% does not exclude heterogeneity.

[w1ab5f0-14] Sensitivity analyses comprised: (i) leave-one-out analyses, reported with both HKSJ and MH intervals, noting that HKSJ intervals with one degree of freedom are uninformative; (ii) the analysis populations reported by the trials; (iii) post hoc bounding scenarios for all randomized patients with unknown outcomes in Elfert et al. [ 17 ] and Praharaj et al. [ 18 ] (Mostafa et al. [ 19 ] had no missing recurrence data)—B1, all patients with unknown outcomes recurred; B2, recurrence imputed for rifaximin patients only (worst case for rifaximin); B3, recurrence imputed for norfloxacin patients only (best case); and B4, unknown outcomes recurred at twice the observed arm-specific rate (an informative-missingness assumption [ 26 ]), together with a deliberately extreme scenario (B5) in which recurrence was also imputed to rifaximin patients who died; and (iv) the composite of recurrence or death, bounded by the range of values compatible with the published data. In Elfert et al. [ 17 ], recurrences were reported among completers and deaths among non-completers, so the two events are disjoint, and only the 17 surviving non-completers are uncertain; in Praharaj et al. [ 18 ], the overlap between SBP and death was not reported, so the composite was bounded between complete overlap and no overlap; Mostafa et al. [ 19 ] reported no deaths. Influence was assessed with a Baujat plot and Cook’s distance in the trial-reported populations. Funnel plots and small-study-effect tests were not performed because they are uninterpretable with three trials. Analyses used R 4.3.3 and metafor version 4.4-0 [ 27 ]; the full code is provided as Supplementary Code .

[w1ab5f0-15] 3. Results 3.1. Study Selection and Characteristics Of 456 records identified (446 from database searches and 10 from citation searching), 17 reports were assessed for eligibility, and seven studies met the inclusion criteria ( Supplementary Figure S1, PRISMA flow ; Table 1 ): four RCTs [ 17 , 19 , 28 , 29 ], one pre-specified RCT subgroup [ 18 ], and two national cohort studies [ 30 , 31 ]. Three trials compared rifaximin with norfloxacin [ 17 , 18 , 19 ]; one compared norfloxacin with placebo [ 28 ]; one compared daily norfloxacin with weekly rufloxacin [ 29 ]; one cohort study compared fluoroquinolone- or trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole-based prophylaxis with none in two databases [ 30 ]; and one propensity-matched cohort compared trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole with ciprofloxacin [ 31 ].

[w1ab5f0-16] 3.2. Risk of Bias Two of the three pooled trials were at high risk of bias: Elfert 2016 [ 17 ] for missing outcome data (recurrence reported for 195 of 262 randomized patients, with differential attrition by arm) and Mostafa 2015 [ 19 ] for the randomization process and post-randomization withdrawals. Praharaj 2022 [ 18 ] raised some concerns (open label, per-protocol subgroup). Elfert 2016 [ 17 ] and Praharaj 2022 [ 18 ] were open-label, and Mostafa 2015 [ 19 ] was single-blind with the blinded party not specified; none was double-blind. Ginés 1990 [ 28 ] and Bauer 2002 [ 29 ] raised some concerns. Silvey 2025 [ 30 ] was at serious risk (confounding by indication) and Osman 2026 [ 31 ] at moderate risk after propensity matching ( Supplementary Tables S5 and S6 ).

[w1ab5f0-17] 3.3. Primary Meta-Analysis: Rifaximin Versus Norfloxacin Across the three trials, 398 patients were randomized (324 in the trial-reported analysis populations); 6 of 204 rifaximin and 31 of 194 norfloxacin patients had documented recurrence. In the primary intention-to-treat analysis, the pooled RR was 0.21 (95% HKSJ CI 0.05–0.89; p = 0.043; I 2 = 0%, τ 2 = 0; Figure 1 ), with study weights of 57.1% (Elfert [ 17 ]), 34.5% (Praharaj [ 18 ]), and 8.4% (Mostafa [ 19 ]). The modified HKSJ interval was 0.03–1.31 ( p = 0.067). The MH RR without continuity correction was 0.19 (0.08–0.44), and the fixed-study-effects log-link model gave an RR of 0.18 (profile-likelihood 95% CI 0.07–0.39). In the trial-reported populations, the pooled RR was 0.22 (HKSJ 0.07–0.69; modified HKSJ 0.04–1.32; MH 0.19, 0.08–0.45). Because τ 2 was estimated as zero, no prediction interval distinct from the confidence interval could be computed.

[w1ab5f0-18] Clinical heterogeneity. The three trials differed in rifaximin dose (800, 1100, and 1200 mg/day), population (Mostafa et al. [ 19 ] enrolled only Child–Pugh C patients with hepatitis C cirrhosis), outcome ascertainment (protocol paracentesis at 2 and 6 months in Elfert et al. [ 17 ] and at 3 and 6 months in Praharaj et al. [ 18 ], versus clinical review without protocol paracentesis in Mostafa et al. [ 19 ]; Table 1 ), analysis population (completers, per protocol, and all randomized, respectively), and setting (two trials from Egypt, one from India); all planned six months of treatment and follow-up. These differences could plausibly modify the relative effect, and with three trials the I 2 statistic has little power to detect them. We therefore do not interpret I 2 = 0% as evidence of homogeneity; the modified HKSJ interval is reported to guard against an underestimated τ 2 , and inconsistency was considered but not downgraded in the GRADE assessment because all three point estimates favored rifaximin.

[w1ab5f0-19] 3.4. Sensitivity Analyses Leave-one-out. In the intention-to-treat analysis, the point estimate remained below unity on omission of each trial (HKSJ RR 0.13, 0.25, and 0.24 omitting Elfert [ 17 ], Praharaj [ 18 ], and Mostafa [ 19 ], respectively), but the HKSJ intervals with one degree of freedom spanned several orders of magnitude and are not interpretable. The MH intervals excluded unity in each case (0.03–0.42, 0.08–0.61, 0.10–0.54). These results show consistency of direction, not robustness of magnitude or significance.

[w1ab5f0-20] Missing outcome data ( Table 2 ). Outcomes were unknown for 17 surviving non-completers in Elfert 2016 [ 17 ] (10 rifaximin, 7 norfloxacin) and for 7 rifaximin patients in Praharaj 2022 [ 18 ]; Mostafa 2015 [ 19 ] had no missing recurrence data. Assuming recurrence in all of these patients (B1), the pooled RR was 0.65 (HKSJ 0.21–2.03; MH 0.59, 0.37–0.94). Assuming recurrence only among rifaximin patients (B2, worst case for rifaximin), it was 0.80 (HKSJ 0.18–3.44; MH 0.73, 0.45–1.17). Under B3 (best case) and B4 (twice the observed arm-specific rate), the MH RRs were 0.15 (0.07–0.35) and 0.23 (0.11–0.49). The deliberately extreme scenario B5, which imputes recurrence to all 28 rifaximin non-completers in Elfert 2016 [ 17 ], including the 18 who died, and to none of the norfloxacin non-completers, gave an MH RR of 1.29 (0.85–1.96) with I 2 = 88%; because it assumes that every rifaximin death and no norfloxacin death was preceded by recurrence, it does not correspond to the primary estimand and is shown as an extreme bound only. The direction of effect therefore favored rifaximin in every scenario compatible with the primary estimand, but statistical significance was lost when unknown outcomes were assumed to be adverse for rifaximin.

[w1ab5f0-21] Recurrence or death. In Elfert 2016 [ 17 ], the composite was 22 to 32 of 131 with rifaximin and 45 to 52 of 131 with norfloxacin, depending on the 17 patients with unknown status (RR 0.42 to 0.71). In Praharaj 2022 [ 18 ], two SBP events and seven deaths occurred among 26 analyzed rifaximin patients and 13 SBP events and 12 deaths among 33 norfloxacin patients, with overlap not reported. Across the resulting bounds, the pooled MH RR for recurrence or death ranged from 0.35 (0.24–0.50) to 0.75 (0.55–1.03), and the REML–HKSJ RR ranged from 0.36 (0.14–0.90) to 0.82 (0.15–4.47); with unknown outcomes assumed event-free, it was 0.41 (0.29–0.58) to 0.46 (0.31–0.67) ( Table 2 ).

[w1ab5f0-22] Influence. In the trial-reported populations, Elfert 2016 [ 17 ] was flagged as influential (Cook’s distance 1.34; hat value 0.57; Supplementary Figure S2 ).

[w1ab5f0-23] 3.5. Secondary Outcomes All-cause mortality was reported in all three pooled trials and was not pooled. In Elfert 2016 [ 17 ], deaths over six months were 18/131 (13.7%) with rifaximin and 32/131 (24.4%) with norfloxacin (RR 0.56, 95% CI 0.33–0.95; randomized population). In the secondary-prophylaxis stratum of Praharaj 2022 [ 18 ], 7 of 26 rifaximin and 12 of 33 norfloxacin patients died (26.9% vs. 36.4%; RR 0.74, 0.34–1.61; analyzed population); these counts are stated in the text of that report, whereas the percentages printed in its Table 2 (28.6% vs. 26.9%) do not correspond to them. Mostafa 2015 [ 19 ] reported no deaths during six months of follow-up. No trial reported resistant infection as an outcome. Elfert 2016 [ 17 ] reported that all culture-positive breakthrough isolates were norfloxacin-resistant (two Escherichia coli with rifaximin; three E. coli , one Pseudomonas , and four Gram-positive cocci with norfloxacin); in Mostafa 2015 [ 19 ], all five breakthrough episodes on norfloxacin were caused by Gram-positive cocci; and Praharaj 2022 [ 18 ] reported the organisms ( E. coli and Stenotrophomonas maltophilia with norfloxacin) without susceptibility data. Elfert 2016 [ 17 ] reported no serious adverse events in either group, and Mostafa 2015 [ 19 ] reported no serious adverse reactions, with minor adverse effects (nausea and headache) confined to the norfloxacin arm. Resistant infection and safety therefore could not be synthesized, and this objective is unmet by the available trials.

[w1ab5f0-24] 3.6. Narrative Synthesis of Other Comparisons Prophylaxis versus none. In the 1990 double-blind trial by Ginés et al., norfloxacin reduced the one-year Kaplan–Meier probability of recurrence from 68% to 20% (crude proportions 5/40 vs. 14/40; RR 0.36, 95% CI 0.14–0.89) [ 28 ]. In the 2009–2019 VA-CDW ( n = 4673) and TriNetX ( n = 6708) cohorts, Silvey et al. found secondary prophylaxis associated with a higher adjusted hazard of recurrence (VA HR 1.63, 95% CI 1.40–1.91; TriNetX HR 1.68) and with fluoroquinolone-resistant organisms in the VA cohort (OR 4.32, 95% CI 1.36–15.83) [ 30 ]. Ginés 1990 [ 28 ] and Silvey 2025 [ 30 ] differ in design, era, comparator, and analysis; they were not pooled, and the contrast between them is descriptive.

[w1ab5f0-25] Alternative regimens. In a propensity-matched TriNetX cohort (2013–2021), trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole was associated with lower recurrence (HR 0.75, 0.72–0.79) and mortality (HR 0.84, 0.80–0.87) than ciprofloxacin [ 31 ]. Bauer et al. found no significant difference between daily norfloxacin and weekly rufloxacin (one-year recurrence 26% vs. 36%) and reported quinolone resistance in all breakthrough isolates [ 29 ]. Silvey 2025 [ 30 ] (TriNetX) and Osman 2026 [ 31 ] overlap in data source and years and were not combined ( Supplementary Table S7 ).

[w1ab5f0-26] The cohort evidence comprised 11,381 patients in Silvey et al. [ 30 ] (VA-CDW 4673; TriNetX 6708) and 22,280 propensity-matched patients in Osman et al. [ 31 ] Because both TriNetX analyses draw on the same federated network over overlapping calendar periods (2009–2019 and 2013–2021), some patients probably contribute to both, so the arithmetic total of 33,661 overstates the number of unique patients and is not reported as a combined sample size.

[w1ab5f0-27] 3.7. Certainty of Evidence Certainty for SBP recurrence with rifaximin versus norfloxacin was very low ( Table 3 ). Starting from high, we downgraded two levels for risk of bias because the trial contributing 57% of the weight (Elfert 2016 [ 17 ]) is at high risk in the domain that drives the result—missing outcome data with differential attrition—and a second trial (Mostafa 2015 [ 19 ]) is at high risk for the randomization process, with none double-blind; and one level for imprecision (three trials, 37 events; modified HKSJ interval including no effect; loss of statistical significance under plausible missing-outcome assumptions). We did not downgrade for inconsistency because all three point estimates favored rifaximin, although clinical heterogeneity was considered ( Section 3.3 ), or for indirectness. Publication bias could not be assessed. We considered a rating of low, with a single downgrade for risk of bias, but did not adopt it because the missing-outcome analyses show that the risk-of-bias concern in the weight-dominant trial can remove statistical significance.

[w1ab5f0-28] 4. Discussion 4.1. Principal Findings In three randomized trials, none of them double-blind, rifaximin was associated with fewer SBP recurrences than norfloxacin, with a pooled point estimate consistent with previous network meta-analyses [ 12 , 13 ]. What this re-appraisal adds is a characterization of how fragile that estimate is: the interval that guards against underestimated heterogeneity crosses unity; the largest trial reports recurrence for only three-quarters of the patients it randomized, with attrition that was substantial, unequal between arms and driven by death; and a second trial excluded seven randomized patients from one arm only. When death is treated as a competing event, every missing-outcome scenario leaves the point estimate below unity, but assuming that the unknown outcomes were adverse for rifaximin removes statistical significance. The composite of recurrence or death, which does not depend on how deaths are handled, also favors rifaximin across all bounds, with an upper confidence limit reaching 1.03 in the least favorable case. The correct summary is therefore that rifaximin may reduce recurrence, with very low certainty—not that its superiority has been demonstrated.

[w1ab5f0-29] As context rather than as evidence on the primary question, contemporary national cohorts associate fluoroquinolone-based secondary prophylaxis with more, not fewer, recurrences and with fluoroquinolone-resistant infection, in contrast to the placebo-controlled trial on which the practice rests [ 28 , 30 ]. These comparisons (prophylaxis versus none; trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole versus ciprofloxacin) do not address the rifaximin–norfloxacin question, and the historical–contemporary contrast is confounded by design and era ( Section 4.3 ).

[w1ab5f0-30] 4.2. Mechanistic Considerations Several mechanisms could account for a benefit of rifaximin, if real. Its minimal absorption concentrates the drug in the gut lumen, where bacterial translocation originates [ 9 , 10 ]; it reduces small-intestinal bacterial overgrowth and has been associated with favorable microbiome remodeling rather than the durable dysbiosis produced by systemic fluoroquinolones [ 11 , 32 ]; and it may impose less selective pressure for fluoroquinolone-resistant Enterobacterales, which are over-represented among breakthrough infections on prophylaxis [ 4 , 29 ]. These mechanisms are biologically plausible but were not tested by the included trials, none of which reported microbiological endpoints.

[w1ab5f0-31] Evidence pointing the other way should be acknowledged. In a prospective cohort of 152 patients, Lutz et al. found no reduction in SBP among patients on rifaximin compared with none, while no patient on systemic prophylaxis developed SBP [ 33 ]. That study was predominantly primary prophylaxis, used ciprofloxacin as the comparator, and had a median observation of weeks, so it does not directly contradict the six-month randomized data; but it cautions against assuming that gut-selective activity translates into peritoneal protection.

[w1ab5f0-32] 4.3. Interpreting the Historical–Contemporary Contrast The divergence between Ginés 1990 [ 28 ] and Silvey 2025 [ 30 ] is striking but has several possible explanations, and our data cannot adjudicate among them. Confounding by indication is the most obvious: patients started on prophylaxis had higher MELD scores and more advanced disease. Differences in comparator (placebo versus no prophylaxis in routine care), outcome ascertainment (trial follow-up versus diagnosis codes), prophylactic agents (norfloxacin versus mixed fluoroquinolones and trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole), and follow-up all contribute. Rising fluoroquinolone resistance is a plausible additional factor, supported by the calendar-time interaction Silvey et al. [ 30 ] observed, the universal quinolone resistance among breakthrough isolates in Bauer et al. [ 29 ], the Gram-positive breakthrough infections on norfloxacin in Mostafa et al. [ 19 ], and the higher resistance prevalence among patients receiving prophylaxis in related VA analyses [ 8 ]. We present this as a hypothesis generated by the contrast, not as a conclusion established by it: era is fully confounded with study design across the available studies, and a meta-regression on calendar year with three data points would be uninformative.

[w1ab5f0-33] 4.4. Implications for Practice and Guidelines Current AASLD, EASL, and APASL guidance positions fluoroquinolones or trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole as first-line secondary prophylaxis [ 5 , 6 , 34 ]. Our findings do not justify changing that guidance. They do support three narrower conclusions. First, the randomized evidence that rifaximin is superior to norfloxacin for secondary prophylaxis is of very low certainty and rests on trials whose outcome reporting is incomplete; guideline panels should weight it accordingly. Second, the evidence base for fluoroquinolone secondary prophylaxis itself predates the current resistance landscape, and contemporary observational signals of harm, while confounded, are consistent across two independent databases and warrant formal re-evaluation [ 8 ]. Third, the choice between agents should be informed by local fluoroquinolone-resistance prevalence, which the included studies did not stratify.

[w1ab5f0-34] 4.5. Future Research An adequately powered, blinded, contemporary randomized trial of rifaximin versus norfloxacin (and ideally trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) for secondary SBP prophylaxis, with intention-to-treat analysis, death handled as a competing event, and microbiological endpoints, remains the clearest need. The difficulty we encountered in reconstructing analysis populations from published reports is itself an argument for participant-level data sharing in prophylaxis trials.

[w1ab5f0-35] 4.6. Strengths and Limitations Strengths include the use of small-sample inference appropriate to three trials, an intention-to-treat estimand with death treated as a competing event, quantitative bounding of the effect under missing-outcome assumptions in every trial with unknown outcomes, a composite outcome that does not depend on how deaths are handled, and verification of all denominators against the participant-flow data of the trial reports.

[w1ab5f0-36] Limitations are substantial. The review was registered retrospectively in PROSPERO (CRD420261501120) on 8 September 2026, after data extraction and the initial analyses; the restriction of pooling to the rifaximin–norfloxacin comparison was data-informed, and the sensitivity analyses, including the estimand adopted during peer review, were specified after the data were examined. The primary meta-analysis contains three trials, none double-blind, with 37 events, and two trials at high risk of bias; two were conducted in a single country. The pooled estimate is sensitive to unreported outcomes, and trial investigators were not contacted for unreported outcome data, so the missing-outcome and composite analyses rely on published reports only. Rifaximin dose ranged from 800 to 1200 mg/day, outcome ascertainment differed between trials, and one contributing unit was a stratum of a larger trial rather than a standalone trial. Safety and resistant infection could not be synthesized because the trials did not report them systematically. With three trials, I 2 = 0% provides little assurance of homogeneity, and publication bias cannot be assessed. The observational evidence is subject to confounding by indication and cannot support causal claims about harm from prophylaxis.

[w1ab5f0-37] 5. Conclusions In patients with cirrhosis and prior SBP, rifaximin may reduce recurrence compared with norfloxacin, but the evidence is very uncertain: the estimate is sensitive to unreported outcomes and loses statistical significance under plausible missing-outcome assumptions, although its direction favored rifaximin in every analysis compatible with the primary estimand. This does not justify changing guideline recommendations; it defines the question that a contemporary, blinded, head-to-head trial with intention-to-treat analysis should answer.

Study screening, data extraction, and risk-of-bias assessment were performed independently by two human reviewers (J.-H.H. and M.-L.C.). Mayjen Prof. Dr. Moestopo 47 Surabaya 60131, Indonesia. Tel: (+62) 81330703762, E-mail: amie.vidyani@fk.unair.ac.id , ORCID: https://orcid.org/0000-0002-7533-5652

[w042d79-3] 2 2024 25 2 2024 83 2 488976 45 53 5 9 2023 5 12 2023 7 12 2023 25 02 2024 03 12 2025 19 01 2026 Copyright © 2024. Korean Society of Gastroenterology. 2024 https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/ This is an open-access article distributed under the terms of the Creative Commons Attribution Non-Commercial License ( http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0 ) which permits unrestricted non-commercial use, distribution, and reproduction in any medium, provided the original work is properly cited. Hepatic hydrothorax is a pleural effusion (typically ≥500 mL) that develops in patients with cirrhosis and/or portal hypertension in the absence of other causes. In most cases, hepatic hydrothorax is seen in patients with ascites. However, ascites is not always found at diagnosis and is not clinically detected in 20% of patients with hepatic hydrothorax. Some patients have no symptoms and incidental findings on radiologic examination lead to the diagnosis of the condition. In the majority of cases, the patients present with symptoms such as dyspnea at rest, cough, nausea, and pleuritic chest pain. The diagnosis of hepatic hydrothorax is based on clinical manifestations, radiological features, and thoracocentesis to exclude other etiologies such as infection (parapneumonic effusion, tuberculosis), malignancy (lymphoma, adenocarcinoma) and chylothorax. The management strategy involves a stepwise approach of one or more of the following: Reducing ascitic fluid production, preventing fluid transfer to the pleural space, fluid drainage from the pleural cavity, pleurodesis (obliteration of the pleural cavity), and liver transplantation. The complications of hepatic hydrothorax are associated with significant morbidity and mortality. The complication that causes the highest morbidity and mortality is spontaneous bacterial empyema (also called spontaneous bacterial pleuritis).

[w042d79-4] Complication Diagnoses Hepatic hydrothorax Manifestation Treatment pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access yes pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript no pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement no pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes pmc-license-ref CC BY-NC INTRODUCTION Hepatic hydrothorax is defined as the accumulation of transudative pleural effusion (generally ≥500 mL) in patients with chronic liver disease and portal hypertension, after excluding other etiologies such as cardiac, pulmonary, renal disorders and so on. 1 , 2

[w042d79-5] Hepatic hydrothorax is an important pulmonary complication in decompensated hepatic cirrhosis, in addition to hepatopulmonary syndrome and portopulmonary hypertension. The prevalence of hepatic hydrothorax is 5-15% in patients with decompensated hepatic cirrhosis and it contributes to 2-3% of the incidence of pleural effusion overall. Hepatic cirrhosis, a chronic liver disease, is the 11th leading cause of death worldwide, contributing to 2.4% of the total deaths worldwide. The data from the Global Burden of Disease Study (GBD 2017) showed that there were 10.6 million cases of decompensated hepatic cirrhosis, with an estimated 1.5 million cases of hepatic hydrothorax worldwide. 3

[w042d79-6] Pleural effusion associated with liver disease was first described by Laennec in the nineteenth century, and Morrow et al. 4 in 1958 were the first to describe a massive right pleural accumulation following cirrhosis. In the majority of cases, hepatic hydrothorax occurs in patients with ascites. However, ascites is not always present at diagnosis and is not clinically detected in 20% of patients with hepatic hydrothorax. 4 , 5 The management of hepatic hydrothorax is complex and requires a multidisciplinary approach. In this article, we aim to comprehensively review the management of hepatic hydrothorax based on recent research.

[w042d79-7] PATHOPHYSIOLOGY The pleural cavity is the space between the internal wall of the thorax and the lung. It is lined by a mesothelial fibrous membrane consisting of parietal and visceral layers. Under normal conditions, the pleural cavity contains a small amount of fluid that serves as a lubricant between the parietal and visceral pleural surfaces. The pleural fluid also plays a role in maintaining a negative pressure between the lung and the lung cavity, allowing effective inhalation and preventing lung collapse. The pleural fluid originates from capillaries on the surface of the parietal pleura that move based on Starling's force and is reabsorbed by the lymphatic stomata on the surface of the parietal pleura, especially in the mediastinal region and diaphragm to maintain pleural fluid volume within the normal range of 0.1-0.3 mL/kg. 6 Fluid can also enter the pleural cavity from the pulmonary interstitium through the visceral pleura or from the peritoneal cavity through small holes in the diaphragm at the rate of 0.5 mL/hour. 7

[w042d79-8] Excessive accumulation of fluid in the pleural cavum may occur due to increased production or decreased absorption, or both. In pleural effusions that result from increased hydrostatic pressure or decreased oncotic pressure in the capillaries (e.g., in hypoalbuminemia), transudative pleural fluid is usually present, while increased capillary permeability (generally due to inflammation) or impaired lymphatic drainage generally results in an exudate. Reabsorption rates may increase up to 40-fold as a physiologic response to additional pleural fluid before fluid begins to accumulate in the pleural cavity. 7 , 8

[w042d79-9] Hepatic hydrothorax occurs due to the direct transfer of fluid from the peritoneal cavity to the pleural cavity through a diaphragmatic defect. These diaphragmatic defects are usually <1 cm in size and commonly occur on the right side (85%), with 13% occurring on the left side and 2% occurring bilaterally. During embryological development, the left side of the diaphragm is more muscular, and the right side is more tendinous due to its proximity to the liver. Hence, embryological defects of the diaphragm are generally more common in the right hemidiaphragm. Diaphragmatic defects associated with hepatic hydrothorax can be classified into 4 forms based on their morphology: Type 1, no obvious defect (9.1-31.7%); Type 2, a small blister on the diaphragm (36.4-41.3%); Type 3, a fenestrated defect on the diaphragm (20.6-72.7%); and Type 4, multiple slits on the diaphragm (1.6-9.1%). The presence of these defects can be confirmed by various modalities, including direct visualization during surgery, doppler ultrasonography, magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), single-photon emission computed tomography (SPECT)-CT, and scintigraphy. 9 - 11

[w042d79-10] Diaphragmatic defects occur in up to 20% of the normal population, but in patients with ascites, the increased abdominal pressure and thinning of the diaphragm due to malnutrition may enlarge the defect. Fluid movement from the abdominal cavity to the pleura is typically one-way, driven by a permanent pressure gradient resulting from intrathoracic negative pressure during the respiratory cycle and intra-abdominal positive pressure. While hepatic hydrothorax usually occurs in patients with ascites, in a minority of cases, it can also develop in patients without ascites. This most commonly occurs in patients with large diaphragmatic defects where ascites may go undetected on physical examination despite the presence of a sizeable pleural effusion. 12

[w042d79-11] Increased abdominal pressure due to ascites and a thinning diaphragm as a result of malnutrition increases the risk of rupture, allowing fluid flow into the pleural compartment, further facilitated by intrathoracic negative pressure. Hypoalbumin and lymphatic duct leakage, as well as cardiac disorders such as diastolic dysfunction and left atrial enlargement, may also be factors contributing to pleural fluid accumulation. 2 , 8

[w042d79-12] CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS The respiratory symptoms of hepatic hydrothorax vary, ranging from cough, pleuritic chest pain, and tightness, to life-threatening respiratory distress. Most effusions are mild to moderate, and only 6% have massive effusions that fill more than half of the hemithorax. Some patients had no symptoms and were incidental findings on radiologic examination, but the majority of cases had symptoms: dyspnea at rest (34%), cough (22%), nausea (11%), and pleuritic chest pain (8%). The severity of symptoms is influenced by the volume of the effusion, the rate of fluid accumulation, and the concomitant presence of other cardiopulmonary disorders. Patients with hepatic hydrothorax will experience dyspnea and/or hypoxia when 1-2 liters of fluid accumulate in the pleural cavity. Hemodynamic disturbances may occur when there is a rapid accumulation of effusion fluid. 2 , 10

[w042d79-13] Patients with hepatic hydrothorax usually also present with other signs of cirrhosis and portal hypertension, such as ascites, spider naevi, asterixis, hepatosplenomegaly, caput medusa, and hepatic encephalopathy. Under certain conditions, patients with hepatic hydrothorax may also develop complications such as acute tension hydrothorax, which cause respiratory distress that can be life-threatening. This could occur when the defective diaphragm ruptures suddenly as a result of increased intra-abdominal pressure, cardiac tamponade, which may occur due to massive effusion. In patients with hepatic hydrothorax with signs and symptoms of fever, pleuritic chest pain, or encephalopathy, it is necessary to consider the possibility of a cavum pleura infection, such as spontaneous bacterial empyema (SBEM) (also called spontaneous bacterial pleuritis [SBPL]). SBEM or SBPL is a complication that can occur due to the migration of a bacterial infection in the peritoneum to the cavum pleura or a bacterial invasion through pleural chest tubes, catheters, or other instruments. 9

[w042d79-14] DIAGNOSIS Hepatic hydrothorax is a diagnosis of exclusion. Therefore, other etiologies of pleural effusion, such as cardiac, pulmonary, pleural disorders, and malignancy, must be ruled out first. Supportive examinations, such as echocardiography and brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) blood tests, can be performed to evaluate cardiac function. CT of the thorax can exclude other causes, such as mediastinal malignancy, and lung or pleural lesions, and abdominal ultrasound can be performed to evaluate the hepatic mass, ascites, and hepatic and portal venous flow. In patients with massive pleural effusions, radiologic examinations should be repeated after diuretic therapy or therapeutic thoracocentesis to assess for pulmonary or pleural disorders covered by the effusion. 4 , 9

[w042d79-15] The diagnosis of hepatic hydrothorax is based on clinical manifestations, radiologic features, and thoracocentesis to exclude other etiologies such as infection (spontaneous bacterial peritonitis [SBP], tuberculosis), malignancy (lymphoma, adenocarcinoma) and chylothorax. 2 Chylothorax can be easily distinguished from hepatic hydrothorax by its milky white appearance and high serum triglyceride content (>110 mg/dL). 13

[w042d79-16] Pleural fluid obtained through thoracocentesis needs to be evaluated for differential cell count, Gram staining, bacterial culture, presence of protein, albumin, lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), fluid pH, and bilirubin concentration. 9 The results of the pleural fluid analysis usually show a transudative effusion with Light's criteria. However, in some cases, there can be misclassification in the analysis wherein the results are termed exudative in patients with hepatic hydrothorax. Such cases can occur when the patient is on diuretic therapy, where there is a contraction of extracellular fluid volume and an increase in the pleural fluid protein and LDH concentrations. In such cases, it is necessary to calculate the serum-pleural fluid albumin gradient (SPAG) which is analogous to the serum- ascites albumin gradient (SAAG), with values >1.1 g/dL indicating a transudative process. In addition to using SPAG, ratio need not unite such as <0.6 g/dL may also be a more accurate method to determine transudative effusion. 13 , 14

[w042d79-17] Other findings usually observed in hepatic hydrothorax include alkaline pH, glucose levels similar to serum glucose, polymorphonuclear (PMN) cells <250 cells/mm 3 , and total protein <2.5 g/dL. SBPL is generally diagnosed if the pleural PMN cells are >500 cells/mm 3 , or >250 cells/mm 3 with a positive culture, after excluding parapneumonic effusion or empyema. Hepatic hydrothorax is diagnosed using international criteria as follows: 1) Gradient of serum albumin to pleural fluid >1.1; 2) Total protein in the pleural fluid <2.5 g/dL or pleural fluid/serum total protein ratio <0.5; 3) Pleural fluid/serum LDH ratio <0.6; and 4) Polymorphonuclear cell count <250 cells/mm 3 ( Table 1 ). 15

[w042d79-18] While the etiology of hepatic hydrothorax remains uncertain, other tests to analyze the pleural fluid such as PCR for mycobacterium, amylase for pancreatic pleural effusion, cytology to rule out malignancy, and evaluation of triglyceride levels to assess chylothorax may be considered. Hepatic hydrothorax typically presents as a right-sided pleural effusion and diagnosis is easy in most cases. 16 When left-sided pleural effusion or hepatic hydrothorax without ascites occurs, scintigraphy may be an option to confirm the relationship between the pleural and peritoneal cavities. Hepatic hydrothorax can also be diagnosed through direct visualization of the diaphragmatic defect with video-associated thoracic surgery (VATS), although this procedure is invasive and should only be performed if the diagnosis remains unclear or if there is a plan to repair the defect. 9

[w042d79-19] TREATMENT The management of hepatic hydrothorax is complex, involving a multidisciplinary approach. The management strategy includes a stepwise approach of one or more of the following five methods: 1) Reducing ascitic fluid production; 2) Preventing fluid transfer to the pleural space; 3) Fluid drainage from the pleural cavity; 4) Pleurodesis (obliteration of the pleural cavity); 5) Liver transplantation. 9

[w042d79-20] 1. Reducing ascitic fluid production 1) Salt restriction The first line of treatment for hepatic hydrothorax is a salt-restricted diet. 17 Salt consumption in patients with hepatic hydrothorax should be limited to less than 2 g/day (<88 mEq). Reduction of salt intake by 10-20%, especially in first-time ascites patients, may lead to a reduction in ascites. 18 Stricter salt restriction may speed up ascites healing but is not recommended as it may lead to malnutrition. 19 Salt restriction should be customized according to each patient's condition. 20

[w042d79-21] 2) Diuretics A low-salt diet is generally not sufficient to reduce hepatic hydrothorax and prevent fluid reaccumulation. Therefore, a combination of loop diuretics and aldosterone receptor antagonists is required. Administration of furosemide and spironolactone can be considered as an option with a maximum weight loss target of 0.5 kg/day in ascites patients without peripheral edema and 1 kg/day in patients with peripheral edema. Diuretic therapy is said to be effective if there is a weight loss of at least 2 kg/week. Spironolactone and furosemide can be started at a dose of 100:40 mg/day and titrated as needed.

[w042d79-22] Body weight, blood pressure, orthostatic symptoms, serum electrolytes, and renal function need to be evaluated in patients on diuretic therapy. If weight loss is inadequate, the urinary sodium/potassium ratio can be assessed, where a ratio of >=1 indicates that the patient has received adequate diuretic treatment but may consume salt beyond the recommended level. In such cases, the level of salt restriction (<2 g/day) needs to be improved. In patients, an inadequate weight loss and urine sodium/potassium ratio <1 indicate inadequate diuretic therapy. The dose of the diuretics needs to be increased with a maximum of 160 mg furosemide and 400 mg spironolactone given daily with a titration interval of 1-2 weeks until a stable and effective regimen can be achieved. 2 , 9 , 21

[w042d79-23] Approximately 20-30% of cirrhotic patients with hepatic hydrothorax develop persistent or recurrent pleural effusions despite salt restrictions and diuretic administration. This condition is referred to as diuretic-resistant hepatic hydrothorax. In certain cases, aggressive diuresis is not feasible due to electrolyte disturbances, renal insufficiency, hemodynamic instability, or precipitation of hepatic encephalopathy. This condition is known as diuretic-intractable hepatic hydrothorax. Both diuretic-resistant and diuretic-intractable hepatic hydrothorax require the consideration of other therapeutic modalities. There is also the term “refractory hepatic hydrothorax” which is defined as pleural effusion with the following criteria: 1) Inadequate response to salt restriction and the maximal dose of diuretics (spironolactone 400 mg/day and furosemide 160 mg/day), and serial thoracocentesis >2 times; or 2) Rapid recurrence after therapeutic thoracocentesis. 15

[w042d79-24] Other medical therapies that can be given are splanchnic and peripheral vasoconstrictors. The vasoconstrictors of choice include octreotide, midodrine, and terlipressin, which increase arterial volume and decrease the activation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS), thereby increasing renal sodium excretion. Octreotide is a somatostatin analog that directly inhibits the RAAS axis, increasing renal plasma flow and natriuresis. In patients with renal failure, a single dose of octreotide may cause adverse renal effects. Therefore, a combination with the alpha-adrenergic agonist midodrine is recommended. In addition, octreotide administration may cause pulmonary and cardiac side effects and also increase the risk of tachyphylaxis. 9

[w042d79-25] Terlipressin is a vasopressin analog that is also used in the management of hepatorenal syndrome (HRS). In patients with hepatitis B- and D-related cirrhosis with hepatic hydrothorax and HRS refractory to thoracentesis and octreotide, terlipressin and albumin may show improvement in signs and symptoms of HRS and hepatic hydrothorax. 9

[w042d79-26] 3) Paracentesis In hepatic hydrothorax patients with permagna ascites, the large-volume paracentesis (LVP) procedure and albumin infusion to prevent associated circulatory disorders (8 g for every liter of ascites fluid removed) showed significant improvement in lung capacity. The ratio of partial pressure of oxygen in arterial blood (PaO 2 ) to the fraction of inspiratory oxygen concentration (FiO 2 ), (P/F) ratio and symptoms of hepatic hydrothorax improved within 2 hours after LVP. The LVP procedure can also alleviate respiratory distress in hepatic hydrothorax patients with ascites and is performed before thoracocentesis. There was a significant increase in total lung capacity and improvement in symptoms within 2 hours after an average of 3.5 liters of ascites fluid was removed by paracentesis. 21 , 22 Paracentesis should be performed in every patient with new-onset ascites as it helps determine the etiology and allows early diagnosis of complications such as SBP. 23

[w042d79-27] 2. Preventing fluid transfer to the pleural space 1) Transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt (TIPS) Fluid accumulation in portal hypertension occurs when the portosystemic gradient (the difference in pressure between the portal vein and the right hepatic vein or inferior vena cava) is increased, generally greater than 12 mmHg (normal ≤5 mmHg). The TIPS reduces portal hypertension by lowering the portosystemic gradient. Although TIPS does not address the cause of portal hypertension, decreasing the portosystemic gradient can address various complications of portal hypertension, including acute and recurrent variceal bleeding, refractory ascites, and refractory hepatic hydrothorax. 2

[w042d79-28] Prior to performing TIPS, comprehensive investigations need to be conducted. This includes laboratory investigations (hepatic and renal profile, electrolytes, coagulation), and calculation of the Model for End-stage Liver Disease (MELD) score, to quantify the severity of the liver disease. The MELD score predicts the 3-month mortality risk in patients with hepatic hydrothorax and is also used in patients requiring hepatic transplantation. It is also necessary to evaluate the cardiac function with an echocardiogram and radiological examination to assess hepatic parenchyma and vascularization. Contraindications for TIPS include the following: Bacteremia, heart failure, severe tricuspid regurgitation, pulmonary hypertension, unresolved biliary obstruction, uncontrolled HE, uncorrected coagulopathy, portal vein thrombosis, hepatic mass, and also patients with a MELD score >18. Complications of TIPS include hepatic encephalopathy, worsening of hepatic function, hepatic ischemia, and shunt stenosis. 2

[w042d79-29] 2) Diaphragm defect repair Surgical closure of the diaphragmatic defect prevents fluid flow into the pleural cavity from the abdomen but has a high mortality rate in patients with decompensated cirrhosis (Child-Turcotte-Pugh [CTP] class C). Surgery may be considered in patients with refractory hepatic hydrothorax who are not TIPS candidates but have low MELD scores. This can be done via video-assisted thoracoscopy (VATS) or open thoracotomy with or without a mesh, as well as laparoscopic surgery. The type of diaphragm defect influences the type of surgery chosen, e.g., type 1 defects are mesh-only, while types 2, 3, 4 are mesh-only with or without suturing. 9

[w042d79-30] 3. Fluid ejection from the pleural cavity 1) Repetitive thoracentesis When medical therapy has not been able to control symptoms, or when renal complications occur due to such therapy, then therapeutic thoracocentesis needs to be performed. Thoracocentesis is performed to relieve respiratory distress or hypoxia. It is recommended that thoracocentesis should not exceed 2 liters in one collection because there is a risk of re-expansion pulmonary edema due to the removal of large amounts of fluid. The intravenous injection of albumin to prevent circulatory dysfunction is generally not required due to the relatively small volume of fluid withdrawn. 22

[w042d79-31] In approximately 27% of cases, the management of hepatic hydrothorax becomes more complex as repeated thoracocentesis is required. In addition, in refractory hepatic hydrothorax, patients who are not candidates for TIPS or transplantation, or in those awaiting transplantation, repeat thoracentesis is the management of choice to manage symptoms. Complications of hemothorax may occur especially in patients with coagulopathic disorders with a PT or aPTT more than double the normal limit, thrombocytopenia (<50,000/mm 3 ), serum creatinine >6 mg/dL, and a high MELD score. 2 , 4 , 9

[w042d79-32] 2) Chest tube insertion Chest tube insertion has a very high complication rate of 88%, including infection, renal failure, and electrolyte disturbances with a 33% mortality rate due to empyema and sepsis. Patients who have a chest tube inserted have twice the risk of an increased length of stay and mortality compared to patients who undergo thoracocentesis. Therefore, chest tube insertion is generally contraindicated in patients with hepatic hydrothorax. However, chest tube insertion can be performed in the management of infected pleural fluid. 9 Chest tube placement should be avoided while other invasive procedures may be considered on a case-by-case basis. 9

[w042d79-33] 3) Indwelling pleural catheter (IPC) insertion. IPC insertion allows intermittent drainage and facilitates pleurodesis. This procedure can be used as a management option for symptomatic malignant pleural effusions and can be a bridging therapy for liver transplantation. Insertion of an IPC allows intermittent drainage at home and can be considered in recurrent hepatic hydrothorax. It can also be chosen as a bridge to transplantation or as a palliative procedure option in patients who cannot undergo liver transplantation. However, the IPC has a risk of complications such as pleural infection, empyema, pneumothorax, and catheter malfunction. 9 , 24

[w042d79-34] 4) Pleurovenous shunting (PVS) A pleurovenous shunt is an indwelling catheter that drains fluid directly from the pleural cavity to the systemic venous circulation. PVS became attractive for treating hepatic hydrothorax as none of the patients required further treatment during a mean follow-up of 13.3 months. However, complications such as shunt failure, pulmonary edema, post-shunt coagulopathy, deep vein thrombosis, infection, and catheter insertion site leakage have been reported and long-term outcomes are not well known. In addition, PVS cannot be performed in cases complicated by infection such as empyema or recurrent SBP. 25

[w042d79-35] A peritoneovesical shunt procedure can also be performed where the device is placed subcutaneously and works to pump excess peritoneal fluid to the bladder where it is eliminated through the urine. The removal of ascites is expected to reduce the formation of hepatic hydrothorax. Both the pleurovenous shunt and peritoneovesical shunt are still not widely used because they are still at the research stage. 6 , 9

[w042d79-36] 4. Pleurodesis Pleurodesis is a procedure that aims to eliminate the space between the parietal and visceral pleura by using chemical irritants or mechanical abrasion to induce fibrosis to prevent recurrent effusion. Pleurodesis can be considered in patients who are not candidates for TIPS or in hepatic hydrothorax patients who are refractory to TIPS and can be a bridging therapy for liver transplantation. Chemical pleurodesis agents that can be used include talcum, tetracycline, doxycycline, bleomycin, povidone-iodine, and pisibanil (OK-432) with or without minocycline. 21

[w042d79-37] 5. Liver transplantation The primary therapy for hepatic hydrothorax is sodium restriction and diuretics, but when medical therapy fails, the only definitive treatment is liver transplantation. 10 Liver transplantation is the definitive therapy for refractory hepatic hydrothorax. Patients with diuretic-resistant, and diuretic-intractable hepatic hydrothorax, history of SBPL, or a high MELD score (>15) need to be referred for evaluation for liver transplantation. 9 Mortality from hepatic hydrothorax is independent of age, gender, MELD-sodium (Na) score, renal function, hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) status, and the occurrence of bleeding varices. Therefore, early liver transplantation should be considered. 26 However, according to Ma et al. 27 in 2022, in a group of hepatic hydrothorax patients not given liver transplantation, those of the male sex and having hepatic encephalopathy had a higher risk of death. 27

[w042d79-38] While liver transplantation is the best treatment option, most patients are not eligible, and a large proportion of those who are eligible die while waiting for a transplant. 6 In difficult cases, the only recourse is surgical treatment targeting the primary closure of the diaphragmatic defect ( Fig. 1 ). 25

[w042d79-39] COMPLICATIONS SBEM or SBPL is a complication of hepatic hydrothorax associated with high morbidity and mortality, with mortality rates estimated at 20%. 28 SBEM is an infection of the pleural effusion in patients with cirrhosis in the absence of underlying pneumonia. SBEM and empyema secondary to pneumonia need to be distinguished because the pathogenesis and treatment for the two conditions are very different, where SBEM can be compared to SBP. However, in a study, as many as 40% of SBEM cases were not associated with SBP and although rare, SBEM can occur in patients with hepatic hydrothorax without clinically detectable ascites. 4

[w042d79-40] SBEM should be suspected in patients with hepatic hydrothorax who present with fever, pleuritic pain, encephalopathy, and/or decreased renal function. Although the mechanism of SBEM is still unclear, it is believed that the process is like SBP, which results from the direct spread of bacteria from the peritoneal cavity. However, transient bacteremia infecting the pleural cavity is also thought to be the mechanism of SBEM, especially in hepatic hydrothorax patients who do not have SBP. The bacteria causing SBEM are usually similar to those causing SBP, namely Escherichia coli , Streptococcus spp , Enterococcus , and Klebsiella , although in certain conditions it can also be caused by uncommon organisms such as Aeromonas veronii .

[w042d79-41] The risk factors for SBEM include high Child-Pugh and MELD scores, SBP, decreased serum albumin, and low pleural fluid total protein values (<1.5 g/dL). The diagnosis of SBEM is based on Lazaridis et al.'s (2004) 29 diagnostic criteria, namely, 1) Positive pleural fluid culture with PMN cells >250 cells/mm 3 or PMN cells >500 cells/mm 3 regardless of culture results; 2) Pleural effusion with a SPAG value >1.1 g/dL; 3) Absence of pneumonia or infectious process seen on radiologic examination. 4

[w042d79-42] The treatment for SBEM is similar to that for SBP. Third-generation cephalosporins (e.g., ceftriaxone and cefotaxime) are the antibiotics of choice in SBEM. Cefotaxime 2 g every 8 hours intravenously has shown good results in patients with cirrhosis. Carbapenems can be used in patients with nosocomial infections and those at high risk of infections by extended- spectrum beta-lactamase (ESBL) producing pathogens, diabetes, and patients who have received cephalosporin therapy in the last 3 months. Therapy should be given as soon as the diagnosis is made and continued for 7-10 days. In patients with slow clinical improvement, repeat thoracocentesis may be performed to assess the response to antibiotics.

[w042d79-43] Chest tube insertion should be avoided in SBEM, except in cases where there is pus in the pleural cavity. A worse prognosis can be expected in patients with SBEM characterized by a high MELD score, requiring treatment in the intensive care unit (ICU), and failure to respond to initial antibiotic therapy. Repeated episodes of SBEM are one of the indications for liver transplant evaluation in hepatic cirrhosis patients with hepatic hydrothorax. 4 , 30

[w042d79-44] Acute tension hydrothorax is a life-threatening complication of hepatic hydrothorax. In this condition, mediastinal compression and cardiopulmonary collapse occur, which can cause shock in patients. The presence of massive quantities of pleural fluids triggers an increase in intra-thoracic pressure and causes a decrease in diastolic filling and cardiac output. Although rare, spontaneous diaphragm rupture can occur in patients with hepatic cirrhosis, causing acute tension hepatic hydrothorax. Spontaneous diaphragm rupture can occur due to increased abdominal pressure triggered by various factors, such as prolonged cough or certain body positions. Diaphragm muscle weakness due to malnutrition or certain genetic conditions can also be a predisposing factor for diaphragm rupture which can trigger acute tension hydrothorax. 31

[w042d79-45] CONCLUSION Hepatic hydrothorax is a complication of liver cirrhosis that requires special attention. Hepatic hydrothorax can present with symptoms that vary from being asymptomatic to cough, chest tightness, pleuritic chest pain, and life-threatening respiratory failure. In addition to clinical manifestations and radiological examination, pleural fluid analysis is required to determine the diagnosis and assess the complications of hepatic hydrothorax. The management of hepatic hydrothorax is quite complex and requires a multidisciplinary approach considering the clinical condition of the patient, the risk of complications that may occur, as well as the availability of health personnel and facilities. Proper diagnosis and therapy are the keys to the successful management of hepatic hydrothorax.
