[w5c6b54-2] 01 6 2025 14 2 2024 81 6 468787 1878 1897 27 03 2024 01 06 2026 01 06 2026 21 05 2025 01 06 2026 This file is available for text mining. It may also be used consistent with the principles of fair use under the copyright law. Introduction Hepatorenal syndrome (HRS) is a rare and highly morbid form of kidney injury unique to patients with decompensated cirrhosis. HRS is a physiologic consequence of portal hypertension, leading to a functional kidney injury that can be reversed by restoring effective circulating volume and renal perfusion. While liver transplantation (LT) is the only definitive “cure” for HRS, medical management with vasoconstrictors and intravenous albumin are a cornerstone of supportive care. Terlipressin, a V1a receptor agonist that acts on the splanchnic circulation, has been used for many years outside the United States for the treatment of HRS. However, its recent Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approval has generated new interest in this population, as a new base of prescribers now work to incorporate the drug into clinical practice. In this article, we review HRS pathophysiology and diagnostic criteria, clinical use of terlipressin and alternative therapies, and identify areas of future research in the space of HRS and kidney injury in cirrhosis. cirrhosis liver failure renal failure portal hypertension dialysis renal replacement therapy vasoconstrictor liver transplantation pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access no pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript yes pmc-prop-open-manuscript yes pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement no pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes Pathophysiology and Background of Hepatorenal Syndrome Early Clinical Descriptions of Hepatorenal Syndrome Early descriptions of the highly morbid link between liver and kidney failure are documented as far back as the 1800s, including references in the historic dropsy literature, early descriptions of renal injury related to biliary surgery (so-called cholemic nephrosis), and a connection between ascites (termed hydro-peritoneum at that time) and renal failure. 1 , 2 By the 1950s and 1960s, this connection was hypothesized to be functional rather than structural , noting a constellation of (a) severe renal vasoconstriction, (b) intact renal parenchyma, and (c) the ability to transiently improve serologic markers of renal function with noradrenaline and volume expansion in these patients. 3 - 7 The potential reversibility of this functional injury was further demonstrated in two New England Journal of Medicine liver transplantation case series, where patients with hepatorenal syndrome (a term that first emerged as a loose clinical descriptor during this era) had reversal of their kidney injury with liver transplantation alone, and conversely, kidneys donated from patients who died with HRS were transplanted into non-cirrhotic patients with end stage renal disease. 8 , 9

[w5c6b54-3] Hemodynamic Abnormalities in Hepatorenal Syndrome Over the ensuing years, the pathophysiology of HRS was elegantly described through a series of clinical observations, culminating in the 1988 publication of the “peripheral arterial vasodilation hypothesis” by Schrier et al . 10 , 11 In short, patients with decompensated cirrhosis have increased intrahepatic vascular resistance, leading to portal hypertension and vasodilation of the splanchnic vasculature. As blood pools in the splanchnic circulation, there is a subsequent decrease in effective arterial blood volume. This results in activation of a cascade of neurohumoral signals aimed at restoring systemic perfusion, including the sympathetic nervous system/catecholamines, renin-angiotensin aldosterone system, and arginine vasopressin (or anti-diuretic hormone, ADH). 12 - 14 In early stages of cirrhosis/portal hypertension, patients are able to compensate for this with a higher cardiac output, a state sometimes described as the hyperdynamic circulation of cirrhosis. 15 , 16 However, as this pathophysiology becomes more advanced, these compensations become maladaptive, resulting in the development of ascites (from excessive sodium retention), hyponatremia (when free water retention exceeds solute reabsorption), and eventually HRS (when renal vasoconstriction becomes so severe, glomerular filtration rate becomes impaired). 17 , 18 A key pathophysiologic transition in an ambulatory patient with ascites and preserved renal function and a patient with ascites who has developed HRS is the loss of the ability to maintain an adequate cardiac output. In a study utilizing serial right heart catheterizations of 66 patients with tense ascites and normal serum creatinine (SCr), patients who later developed HRS had lower cardiac output relative to those who did not, and a decrease in cardiac output correlated with increases in renin/aldosterone/catecholamine levels, the development of hyponatremia and HRS. 19 This inability to maintain a compensatory increase in cardiac output is a consequence of not just the decrease in preload from pooling of blood volume in the splanchnic circulation, but also the development of impaired diastolic function, reduced cardiac contractility, and altered electrophysiologic signaling, resulting in a decreased ability to respond to physiologic stress—collectively, a syndrome described as cirrhotic cardiomyopathy. 20 - 22 While intra-abdominal pressures are rarely documented in studies of HRS, it is possible that when intraabdominal pressure exceeds central venous pressure (as can be the case in patients with tense ascites), the resulting renal venous congestion also contributes to the hemodynamic alterations and kidney injury discussed above. 23 , 24

[w5c6b54-4] Systemic Inflammation in Hepatorenal Syndrome For many years, HRS was described largely in hemodynamic terms. However, over the last two decades, there has been increased acknowledgement that systemic inflammation may also contribute to the pathogenesis of HRS. 25 , 26 The mechanism by which systemic inflammation interacts with the hemodynamic pathophysiology of HRS is not clear, be by enhancing systemic vasodilation, by causing direct inflammatory damage to the kidneys, or a combination of the two. Regardless, elevated levels of pro-inflammatory cytokines, including TNF-alpha, IL-6, and IL-8 have been described in HRS and syndromes associated with HRS like acute-on-chronic liver failure (ACLF) and have been documented as early as grade I ascites, earlier in the natural history of portal hypertension/HRS. 27 - 31 Combined with low effective arterial volume, secondary inflammatory insults, such as overt infections like spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) 32 or subclinical levels of gut bacterial translocation detectable at the DNA or RNA level, 33 - 35 likely are synergistic in precipitating a more dramatic decline in renal function in these at-risk patients. 36 - 38 As a case example, the use of prophylactic antibiotics for the prevention of spontaneous bacterial peritonitis not only decreases infection rates but also rates of HRS, suggesting that inflammation and infection play a meaningful role in the development of HRS. 39 Intravenous albumin administration may also interact with systemic inflammation in this population (see Role of Intravenous Albumin below). 40 - 42

[w5c6b54-5] Figure 1 synthesizes both hemodynamic and inflammatory contributors to HRS.

[w5c6b54-6] Future research: The pathophysiology of HRS-AKI has evolved to represent the culmination of hemodynamic and inflammatory contributions. However, further specificity into the triggers of HRS incidence and drivers of HRS reversal are needed. Currently, few preventative strategies or known triggers exist. For example, there remains equipoise as to when to discontinue non-selective beta blockers—the specific hemodynamic range where the benefits outweigh the risk remain to be delineated 43 , 44 Similarly, advancements in our understanding of the inflammatory drivers of HRS progression are lacking, as initial trials have yet to yield effective therapeutic strategies for HRS. 45 Granularity into the specific pathophysiologic pathways will inform the evolution of future preventative and therapeutic interventions.

[w5c6b54-7] Diagnosis and Definitions of Hepatorenal Syndrome Evolution of the Diagnostic Criteria While definitions and terminology have evolved over time, one immutable feature of HRS is that it has always been a “syndrome,” representing a heterogenous pattern of injury that is not easily established by tissue pathology or simple/objective diagnostic tests: a so-called “diagnosis of exclusion.” Though it preceded the 1988 peripheral arterial vasodilation hypothesis, the first diagnostic criteria for HRS was published in 1979 following an international HRS symposium in Sasseri, Italy. 46 These criteria were later updated by the International Club of Ascites (ICA) in 1996, 47 2007, 48 and 2015. 49 These ICA definitions have served as the basis for more recent societal guidelines over the years, 50 - 52 thus we will focus on the ICA criteria in this discussion. The evolution of the HRS diagnostic criteria is summarized in Table 1 . Importantly, each set of criteria establish four specific domains which serve to aid the clinician in excluding alternative causes of kidney injury in cirrhosis. These include: (1) defining the population at risk as having advanced liver disease (specifically, cirrhosis with ascites), (2) setting a creatinine-based threshold to quantify the extent of kidney injury, (3) providing a volume challenge to exclude hypovolemic/prerenal causes, and (4) using clinical history and objective findings to exclude structural/parenchymal kidney injury.

[w5c6b54-8] Current Diagnostic Criteria HRS is divided into two broad categories: hepatorenal syndrome-acute kidney injury (HRS-AKI) and hepatorenal syndrome-non acute kidney (HRS-nAKI), a term later proposed in an ICA-adjacent position paper. 53 HRS-AKI has a relatively straightforward definition, and maps closely to the historical HRS type 1 term used to describe the acute and more severe form of HRS. Notably, in 2015, a relative change in SCr that follows KDIGO/AKIN conventions for AKI replaced the absolute SCr threshold of 2.5 mg/dL seen in the previous ICA-HRS definition, allowing for more sensitive detection and earlier intervention of HRS. 54 - 57 This was a welcome update, as multiple analyses have demonstrated that even small increases in serum creatinine (stage 1 AKI is defined as ≥0.3 mg/dL increase within 48 hours or ≥50% from baseline within 7 days) are associated with worse outcomes in cirrhosis, and several studies have shown that vasoconstrictors like terlipressin are more likely to reverse the kidney injury of HRS when initiated at lower SCr values. 54 , 58 - 61 Despite SCr’s strong performance in prognostic models like the Model for End Stage Liver Disease (MELD) score, 62 , 63 there are concerns about its accuracy in cirrhosis. Many patients with cirrhosis have sarcopenia. 64 Because it is a product of muscle catabolism, SCr may be falsely low in this population, and thus overestimate glomerular filtration rate (eGFR), particularly in women. 65 - 68 In the general nephrology literature, cystatin C, a product of cell cycle metabolism not subject to this limitation, has been increasingly incorporated into eGFR formulas and clinical practice to address this limitation. 69 - 73 Several studies have demonstrated that cystatin C may better estimate kidney function in cirrhosis as well. 74 - 77 However, current guidelines do not yet recommend routine measurement of cystatin C in this population.

[w5c6b54-9] The definition of the subacute HRS-nAKI, which maps less discretely to the historical HRS type 2 term, encompasses hepatorenal syndrome-acute kidney disease (HRS-AKD, or HRS that lasts more than 7 but less than 90 days), and hepatorenal syndrome-chronic kidney disease (HRS-CKD, or HRS that lasts more than 90 days). 53 Our understanding of HRS-nAKI is far more limited than HRS-AKI. Nearly all the literature on terlipressin and other vasoconstrictors (discussed below) focus on HRS type 1 or HRS-AKI. 78 , 79 Additionally, there is only one International Classification of Diseases (ICD) code for hepatorenal syndrome, without distinction between HRS-AKI and HRS-nAKI, making it difficult to quantify the incidence of the latter. 80 We do know that HRS-AKI is rare, making up only 10-15% of all cases of AKI in cirrhosis, 80 - 83 but do not know what percentage of patients with cirrhosis and CKD have HRS-CKD. The HRS diagnostic criteria further limit detection of HRS-nAKI. Because the definition of HRS is built around an acute injury, most of these patients are hospitalized and can readily receive 2 days of an intravenous (IV) albumin volume challenge, which helps rule out a hypovolemia-related injury (as HRS should not readily reverse with a volume challenge) and fulfills one of the ICA diagnostic criteria. However, patients with HRS-AKD or HRS-CKD are more likely to be in the outpatient setting, without access to IV albumin, and thus cannot technically be diagnosed with HRS using these terms. Further, it is often difficult to distinguish acute from chronic parenchymal injury in these patients. For example, the absence of hematuria and proteinuria are part of the ICA-HRS criteria, but it is certainly possible for a patient with CKD from an organic cause, such as diabetic nephropathy with chronic hematuria or proteinuria, to suffer from superimposed HRS-AKI. As the life-expectancy of patients with cirrhosis increases and the incidence of metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD, formerly non-alcoholic fatty liver disease or NALFD) rises, comorbidities like diabetes, hypertension, and resultant organic CKD in this population further make the diagnosis of HRS challenging. 26 , 84 , 85

[w5c6b54-10] Hepatorenal Syndrome versus Acute Tubular Necrosis One of the main difficulties in making the diagnosis of HRS-AKI is distinguishing it from acute tubular necrosis (ATN), another common form of persistent AKI in cirrhosis that does not respond to a volume challenge. 83 , 86 , 87 While guidelines do enable this on clinical grounds (the presence of shock or exposure to nephrotoxins exclude a diagnosis HRS-AKI, as these would be more consistent with ATN), objective tests to discriminate the two are absent from the ICA-HRS criteria. 49 Three objective tests of tubular function are worth discussion: presence of granular casts on urinary sediment, fractional excretion of sodium (FeNa) and of urea (FeUrea), and novel biomarkers of tubular damage. Granular casts were intentionally left out of the 2015 ICA-HRS criteria due to their limited sensitivity/specificity to diagnose ATN and because of the potential for overlap between HRS-AKI and concurrent forms of tubular insults, such as bile cast nephropathy, whose pathogenic mechanism in AKI and cirrhosis has long been debated. 88 - 92 HRS-AKI is an extremely sodium/solute avid state, so an undetectable urine sodium (<10 mEq/L), FeNa (<1%), or FeUrea (<30%) are supportive of this diagnosis. 47 , 87 , 93 Urine sodium excretion was present in the 1979 Sasseri conference and 1996 ICA criteria, but later removed due to concern that concurrent diuretic use may falsely elevate values, and thus lead to inaccurate exclusion of HRS. 48 Novel urinary biomarkers of tubular damage, such as interleukin-18 (IL-18), kidney injury marker-1 (KIM-1), liver fatty acid binding protein (L-FABP), and neutrophil gelatinase-associated lipocalin (NGAL) are markers with high specificity for direct tubular damage. 87 , 94 - 97 These markers also perform well in discriminating between ATN and non-ATN kidney injury in cirrhosis, though are not widely available, as none are approved for clinical use in the United States. 98 In particular, urinary NGAL has outperformed other biomarkers in differentiating AKI phenotype in research cohorts in the U.S. and Europe, 99 , 100 and, in one European practice setting, was able to predict response to vasoconstrictors in HRS-AKI. 101 However, optimal outpoints for NGAL in cirrhosis are not yet well validated. 102 , 103 Additionally, prerenal AKI and ATN, the main differential diagnoses for HRS-AKI, are themselves clinical syndromes that lack a definitive objective test or universal definition. Some biomarker studies have attempted to define ATN based on urine sodium and urine osmolality, 100 , 101 but these definitions may have low sensitivity, leaving some patients in a “miscellaneous” or “unclassifiablc” status, which is not diagnostically useful for the clinician.

[w5c6b54-11] Future research: It is likely that HRS criteria will continue to evolve over time as we incorporate new discoveries around its pathophysiology and potential mechanisms of injury. It is vital to acknowledge that the key to defining HRS is to identify patients who are likely to benefit from HRS-directed therapies. In concert with the clinical syndrome established by HRS guideline definitions, these patients have three main physiologic needs that should be identified to enrich future trials and improve chances of treatment success: (1) restoration of kidney perfusion on a macro-circulatory level (vasoconstrictors and volume expansion address this lesion), (2) functional renal tubules (namely, better discrimination of HRS from ATN, as we presume that damaged tubules will not respond to improvement in renal perfusion), and (3) intact microcirculation (i.e., lack of inflammation/endothelial leak that would further impair filtration even in the presence of improved macro-circulation). 104 Research should focus on the optimal implementation of novel biomarkers (e.g., outpoints for discriminating HRS from ATN using biomarkers like NGAL), defining the utility of widely available tests (e.g., FeNa, FeUrea), and investigation into markers of clinically relevant outcomes rather than diagnosis (e.g., AKI reversal, mortality). The utility of improved measurement of kidney function, potentially by incorporating cystatin C measurements into research in AKI and cirrhosis, as well as establishing measures of renal functional reserve, may prove to be more sensitive and specific in measuring AKI incidence and severity. 105 - 107 Refinement of the definitions to HRS-nAKI, HRS-AKD, and HRS-CKD (and the creation of new ICD codes to reflect the current disease classification) will assist in quantifying their incidence, more accurately defining the population burden. Consensus definitions of prerenal AKI and ATN will better homogenize future prospective studies.

[w5c6b54-12] Terlipressin and the Treatment of Hepatorenal Syndrome Supportive Care for AKI in Cirrhosis In addition to pharmacological therapy for HRS-AKI (discussed below), general supportive measures to avoid secondary kidney insults and prevent AKI progression should be employed. These measures include: addressing metabolic derangements such as hyperkalemia and metabolic acidosis, optimization of volume status, evaluation and treatment of common precipitants such as infection, discontinuation/avoidance of nephrotoxic agents (e.g., nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs and iodinated contrast material), appropriate renal dosing of medications (e.g., vancomycin, gabapentin, etc.), and appropriate supportive measures for other complications of decompensated cirrhosis. 49 , 56 Though diuretics are not inherently nephrotoxic, they are often discontinued in the setting of AKI in cirrhosis, as they can potentially precipitate further intravascular volume depletion and worsen hypotension. However, loop diuretics should be considered for the management of pulmonary edema and/or suspected intravascular volume overload, especially in patients experiencing respiratory distress. In patients with tense ascites requiring therapeutic paracentesis, the amount of ascites drainage that would pose a risk of AKI progression remains uncertain. Extrapolating from previous studies of hospitalized patients, a 5-liter limit with appropriate albumin infusion (6-8 grams per liter removed) may be a reasonable approach to avoid the development of post paracentesis circulatory dysfunction. 108 - 112 Attention should also be given to optimizing nutritional status as hospitalized patients with cirrhosis are often malnourished and/or sarcopenic. 64 , 113 In AKI, malnutrition can lead to increased morbidity, metabolic derangements, length of stay, and mortality. 114 General dietary recommendations for hospitalized patients with cirrhosis include a protein intake of 1.2-1.5 grams/kg/day and targeting at least 22 kcal/kg/day. 113 Restriction of protein intake with the aim of preventing or delaying renal replacement therapy (RRT) should be avoided. 56

[w5c6b54-13] Future Research: The supportive efforts to allow for HRS reversal require further tuning. Better methods of noninvasive volume assessment will enable better diagnosis and management. The safety of loop diuretics with terlipressin in the setting of volume overload as well as moderate/large volume paracentesis with adequate albumin administration are both still unclear. Further study of the association between nutritional status/sarcopenia and HRS-AKI reversal should expand on the existing literature. 115 Clearly, beyond the application of known therapeutics, there are potential supportive pathways that will facilitate HRS reversal.

[w5c6b54-14] Terlipressin Background and Mechanism of Action The positive effect of vasopressin on kidney perfusion in patients with cirrhosis was first recognized over 50 years ago and was first studied in patients with HRS in 1998. 116 , 117 Terlipressin is a synthetic vasopressin analogue. It is a prodrug whose active metabolite, lysine-vasopressin, is steadily released over 4 to 6 hours, making it easier to administer than vasopressin, which has a half-life of only 20 minutes. 118 Its main mechanism of action involves binding to V1 receptors on vascular smooth muscle, which causes vasoconstriction, and to V2 receptors at the distal nephron, which mediates an antidiuretic effect. 119 Importantly, lysine-vasopressin has twice the affinity for V1 as vasopressin, and a 6 fold greater affinity for V1 than V2. 120 Although it is considered a non-selective agonist of both V1 and V2, lysine-vasopressin has greater selectivity for V1, which results in the desired splanchnic vasoconstriction, thereby countering the splanchnic vasodilation that occurs in portal hypertension. 121 Terlipressin also results in increased systemic vascular resistance and decreased cardiac output. 118

[w5c6b54-15] Evidence Behind Terlipressin Terlipressin has been studied in 11 randomized trials compared to albumin alone and 8 randomized trials compared to other vasoconstictors for the treatment of HRS. 60 , 78 , 79 , 122 It has been the mainstay of treatment of HRS in Europe and in Asia for over a decade (approved by the European Medicines Agency in 2010), and has recently been approved by the FDA for use in the United States in 2022. There are three randomized placebo-controlled trials of terlipressin versus albumin alone for the treatment of HRS that were undertaken predominantly in North America and were used for the basis of US approval ( Table 2 ): OT-0401 (published in 2008), 123 REVERSE (published in 2016), 124 and CONFIRM (published in 2021). 60 The lengthy process that led to the FDA approval of terlipressin highlights two important points. First, the populations enrolled and primary outcomes (variations of treatment response) were heterogeneous across these three trials, leading to some inconsistent findings. Second, in part due to HRS’s rare incidence, these trials were not powered to detect a difference in mortality, and in fact, none showed improvement in this important secondary outcome. As such, approval was delayed until the largest and most recent trial (CONFIRM) had its results thoroughly analyzed.

[w5c6b54-16] All three trials used an intermittent bolus form of terlipressin and included patients meeting an older definition of type 1 HRS, with a SCr ≥2.5 mg/dL, except for CONFIRM, which included patients with a SCr ≥2.25 mg/dL. In OT-0401 in which 112 patients were included, the primary outcome was defined as two consecutive SCr ≤1.5 mg/dL separated by 48 hours by day 14 of treatment without renal replacement therapy or transplant. While there was no significant difference in the proportion of patients reaching the primary outcome in the terlipressin group compared to placebo (25% vs 13% respectively, p=0.093), a significantly higher proportion of patients treated with terlipressin achieved HRS reversal, defined as at least one SCr ≤1.5 mg/dL during treatment period, compared to placebo (34% vs 13% respectively, p=0.008). The FDA issued a Complete Response Letter (CRL) in 2009, in essence requesting another adequate and well controlled trial that would demonstrate safety and efficacy of terlipressin. Subsequently, the REVERSE trial included 196 patients with HRS, with a similar primary outcome to OT-0401. However, only 20% of patients treated with terlipressin reached the primary outcome, vs. 13% placebo group (p=0.22). Finally, CONFIRM, the third and most recent trial, enrolled 300 patients with HRS. The primary outcome, described as “verified reversal of HRS”, was defined as two consecutive SCr ≤1.5 mg/dL separated by 2 hours by day 14 of treatment followed by at least 10 days of survival without renal replacement therapy (RRT). This is the first trial to include a component of survival in the definition of the primary outcome, albeit an extremely short-term one. Here, there was a significant difference noted between groups, with 32% vs 17% reaching the primary endpoint in the terlipressin vs placebo groups, respectively (p=0.006). Following this, the FDA initially issued another CRL for terlipressin in 2020 due to uncertainty related to adverse events, but ultimately moved forward with approval in 2022, with a boxed warning on its use in certain groups (see below).

[w5c6b54-17] The lack of survival benefit associated with terlipressin in these three trials has been the source of much controversy. Neither 180-day mortality for OT-0401, nor 90-day mortality for the REVERSE and CONFIRM trials, differed significantly between treatment groups—OT-0401: Terlipressin 57% vs Placebo 62%; REVERSE: 42% vs 45%; CONFIRM: 51% vs 45%.

[w5c6b54-18] Clinically, terlipressin may reverse HRS, but does not alter the natural history of underlying cirrhosis, so that even if patients respond to treatment, they may suffer further life-threatening complications of end-stage liver disease, such as encephalopathy, infection, or even recurrent HRS. Statistically, none of these trials were powered to evaluate mortality. Interestingly, in a meta-analysis including 7 randomized controlled trials with over 500 patients, but pre-dating the CONFIRM trial, terlipressin compared to albumin alone was associated with a lower relative risk for mortality (0.78 [95%CI 0.63-0.98]). 79 In the case of the CONFIRM trial, the lack of mortality difference may be explained by adverse events associated with terlipressin (see below), as well as the inclusion of a patient population that was late in the natural history of their liver disease, thus conferring an overall poor prognosis. More specifically, ~20% of patients had ACLF grade 3—very severe disease—and the average pre-treatment SCr was 3.5 mg/dL—high enough that the expected response rate was substantially lower; thus many CONFIRM patients may not have been most likely to benefit. 61 , 125 , 126

[w5c6b54-19] Outcomes other than HRS reversal and mortality are important metrics in this population as well. In a sub-study of the CONFIRM trial looking at patients admitted to the ICU and alive at the end of their stay, the length of ICU stay was significantly shorter for those who received terlipressin compared to those who did not (4 vs. 11 days respectively). 127 Furthermore, in the peri-LT setting, terlipressin response pre-LT was associated with significantly lower post-transplant CKD (31% vs 65%) and lower requirements for RRT (0% vs 20%) compared to terlipressin non-response. 128

[w5c6b54-20] Future research: There remains ample opportunity to determine the terlipressin candidates who are most likely to respond to therapy. For instance, recent changes in ICA definitions of HRS-AKI hopefully will translate to earlier terlipressin initiation in the HRS disease course, at a time where intervention will improve the probability of response. Most importantly, future studies should investigate the real-world effectiveness of terlipressin—study designs that should allow the power to investigate survival outcomes. Lastly, there remains the opportunity to investigate the utility of terlipressin for other diagnoses (e.g., non-HRS AKI with hypotension).

[w5c6b54-21] How to Prescribe Terlipressin Terlipressin represents the only FDA-approved medication for the treatment of HRS-AKI. Of note, outside the United States (and in all published literature), terlipressin is dosed in 1 mg increments, which is equivalent to 0.85 mg terlipressin acetate, as it is listed on the FDA label. Terlipressin should be dosed using a discrete algorithm ( Figure 2 ). 60 , 129 This algorithm involves several key steps: (1) diagnosis of HRS-AKI; (2) assessment of pre-treatment comorbidities (ACLF grade, volume status, oxygenation, ongoing ischemia, baseline electrocardiogram and echocardiogram, chest X-ray, and a pre-treatment SCr); (3) initiation of terlipressin via an intravenous (IV) bolus of 0.85 mg terlipressin acetate every 6 hours until Day 4 of therapy; (4) at Day 4, assessment of the degree of treatment response in SCr, at which time dosing is either maintained, increased to 1.7 mg IV every 6 hours, or discontinued based on changes in SCr. These steps should be followed with the addition of the continuous reappraisal of volume status, adverse events (e.g., hypoxemia, ischemia), and a daily assessment of treatment response (i.e., decrease in SCr). Importantly, if there is no treatment response after day 4, defined as no improvement or an increase in SCr from initiation, then treatment with terlipressin should be discontinued, and alternative therapies (e.g., norepinephrine, renal replacement therapy) should be considered. These steps represent the current FDA-approved administration of terlipressin; however, throughout the years of use, alternative dosing strategies have been investigated. Overall, patience is required when assessing response to terlipressin, especially when starting at high SCr values, as improvement can take several days.

[w5c6b54-22] Bolus vs. Continuous Infusions: In a randomized trial of 78 patients with HRS comparing a terlipressin infusion (2 to 12 mg/day IV) to terlipressin bolus (0.5 to 2 mg every 6 hours IV), investigators found that the continuous infusion was better tolerated (adverse events: 35% vs. 62%, p<0.025) with a lower mean daily effective dose in the continuous group (2.23 vs. 3.51 mg/day, p<0.05) yielding similar HRS reversal rates. 130 Continuous dosing strategies are generally favored in current practice outside the United States and have been used in two non-US terlipressin trials comparing continuous terlipressin to other vasoconstrictors. 122 , 131 Outside one active trial that has not yet been published ( NCT04460560 ), there have been no studies looking at continuous terlipressin dosing in US patients.

[w5c6b54-23] Metric-based Dosing: To date, there have been two metrics tested to target the dose escalation of terlipressin. The first is SCr, where studies have increased dosing every 48-96 hours based on the changes in SCr during the preceding period, as is recommended on the FDA label. The second is mean arterial pressure (MAP). Higher MAP, either as a relative increase of 5-15 mmHg or exceeding an absolute threshold, has been associated with HRS-AKI reversal. 132 - 135 Some experts have advocated for a titration of terlipressin to a targeted increase in MAP; however, this strategy has not been tested in clinical trials and therefore is not recommended in clinical practice. 26 No metric-based de-escalation strategies of terlipressin have yet been described.

[w5c6b54-24] Treatment Targets: All clinical trials of terlipressin have utilized improvement in SCr as a treatment target, though the exact threshold has varied. Most trials have used two consecutive SCr measurements <1.5 mg/dL, in keeping with older ICA guideline definitions of response for HRS type 1. 48 , 60 , 123 , 124 Most recently, complete response (sometimes termed HRS reversal) was defined as improvement in SCr to within 0.3 mg/dL of a recent outpatient baseline SCr and partial response was defined as regression of AKI stage to a final SCr ≥0.3 mg/dL from this outpatient baseline SCr. 49 , 136 , 137 While FDA labeling suggests treatment until two consecutive SCr are <1.5 mg/dL, if an outpatient baseline SCr is available, it is reasonable to treat until achieving newer definition of complete response. To date, complete response has only been used as an endpoint in one terlipressin trial. 122

[w5c6b54-25] Duration of Therapy: Duration of terlipressin treatment varies depending on clinical setting. In addition to stopping terlipressin once the SCr target mentioned above has been reached, terlipressin should be stopped if a patient develops a serious adverse reaction (see next section), progresses to receive either RRT or liver transplantation, has received the maximum recommended treatment course, or fails to show improvement in SCr after several days of receiving the maximally tolerated dose. FDA-labeling for bolus dosing only recommends a single dose assessment Day 4 of therapy, though if using continuous dosing, the dose may be gradually increased every 48 hours until response is demonstrated. In the 3 North American terlipressin trials, mean duration of terlipressin treatment was between 5-6 days, with a maximum duration of 14 days, 60 , 123 , 124 though there are small case series using continuous terlipressin infusion over longer periods as a bridge to transplant in patients who experienced recurrence of HRS, which has an incidence estimated around 20-30% following terlipressin discontinuation. 25 , 51 , 115 , 138 , 139

[w5c6b54-26] Future Research: Several research questions remain around optimizing terlipressin use: what are the best pharmacodynamic metrics (e.g., MAP, SCr) to titrate dosing? What is the optimal endpoint (e.g., complete response, partial response, or an absolute SCr threshold) for treatment success? What is the best dosing strategy between continuous and bolus approaches? How should patients at risk for adverse events be best identified? Are there other populations (e.g., patients with HRS-CKD, patients with recurrent HRS-AKI) that may benefit from terlipressin? Clearly, there are many opportunities to optimize the utilization of terlipressin, and large, multi-center cohorts are needed for this collaborative research.

[w5c6b54-27] Terlipressin’s Adverse Effects As with any supportive therapy, it is important to balance terlipressin’s adverse events with its clinical effect. Table 2 describes endpoints, outcomes, and serious adverse events (SAEs) in the 3 North American trials. 60 , 123 , 124 Overall, most patients experienced an adverse event (AE) during treatment (terlipressin 88% vs. placebo 89%). The high frequency of AEs among both groups highlights the severity of illness in this population. The AEs that occurred ≥2% more often with terlipressin compared placebo included: ischemia-associated events: 4.5% [95%CI 1.6%-7.4%] risk difference between groups; respiratory disorders (e.g., respiratory failure, dyspnea, pulmonary edema): 14.2% [95%CI 3.3%-25.1%] risk difference; and gastrointestinal disorders (e.g., abdominal pain, diarrhea, nausea): 12.1% [95%CI 0.4%-23.8%] risk difference.

[w5c6b54-28] The most notable serious adverse event associated with terlipressin was respiratory failure, which occurred in 12.6% of terlipressin-treated patients (vs. 7.6% placebo) across the three trials, with a larger discrepancy seen in the CONFIRM trial specifically (14.0% vs. 5.1%). This safety signal has been hypothesized as a culmination of factors, including the underlying critical illness of the population, the iatrogenic effect of over-resuscitation leading to pulmonary edema (notably, the total albumin given in CONFIRM was 521 g during the index admission, a marked increase in volume administration from prior trials), and downstream consequences of terlipressin-induced vasoconstriction, which lead to relative increases in systemic vascular resistance and cardiac strain. 140 - 142 Post hoc analysis of the CONFIRM trial demonstrated that patients with more advanced underlying cirrhosis and multisystem organ dysfunction on presentation (namely, ACLF grade 3) were more likely to experience respiratory failure and were at increased risk of death from it. 143 Terlipressin currently has an FDA boxed warning advising against its use in patients with ACLF grade 3 or underlying hypoxia (oxygen saturation <90%), as well as mandating continuous oxygen saturation monitoring during treatment. We also advocate for regular assessment of volume status, as well as reduction/discontinuation of intravenous albumin and/or initiation of diuretics if pulmonary edema or intravascular volume overload is suspected.

[w5c6b54-29] Other important SAEs include ischemic events (2.9% terlipressin vs. 0.4% placebo). Of 10 ischemic SAEs in those on terlipressin in the CONFIRM trial, there were 4 cases of intestinal ischemia. Ischemia is a rare event, but the utilization of terlipressin should be avoided in those with ongoing coronary, peripheral, or mesenteric ischemia, as the administration of a vasoconstrictor will likely exacerbate these conditions. Gastrointestinal SAEs were commonly seen in these trials (13.8% vs. 7.2%) and include hemorrhage, abdominal pain, diarrhea, and nausea/vomiting. Generally, these abdominal symptoms often improve with dose reduction, transition from bolus to continuous dosing strategies, or discontinuation of terlipressin.

[w5c6b54-30] Alternative Vasoconstrictors and Treatments Several other vasoconstrictors have been compared to terlipressin and have been used as off-label treatments to treat HRS in clinical practice. The combination of midodrine, octreotide and albumin was evaluated in a single randomized, open-label study of 49 patients in Italy, which demonstrated that terlipressin resulted in higher rates of HRS reversal than midodrine and octreotide. 131 There have been 5 single-center, randomized clinical trials comparing terlipressin against norepinephrine (including one pilot trial in HRS type 2). 122 , 144 - 147 There were no mortality differences in these 5 trials, 78 and similar HRS reversal rates between agents were seen in all but one of the trials. This latter study favored terlipressin over norepinephrine in a population with HRS-AKI and ACLF. 122 One additional challenge of the use of norepinephrine is the need for central access and high level of monitoring during administration, norepinephrine typically requires intensive care unit admission. 148 Most societal guidelines recommend terlipressin as the first-line agent for HRS-AKI, followed by norepinephrine if terlipressin is not available or otherwise contraindicated. 50 - 52 Off-label midodrine/octreotide should be reserved for situations where terlipressin and norepinephrine are not available. We advise against the practice of trialing midodrine/octreotide and moving to terlipressin as a second-line agent, as midodrine/octreotide have very low rates of treatment success, and delaying initiation of terlipressin is associated with lower rates of HRS reversal. 60 , 61 , 131

[w5c6b54-31] Future Research: Novel therapeutic agents aimed at improving renal perfusion are needed for the treatment of HRS-AKI. The adjunct role of decompression of portal hypertension through shunts (such as TIPS) requires further study for the treatment of HRS. 149 At the time of this review article, there are several ongoing trials in HRS, including using TIPS in conjunction with terlipressin ( NCT05346393 ), 149 OCE-205, a mixed V1a receptor agonist/antagonist ( NCT05309200 ), 150 and R2R01, a relaxin agonist ( NCT05875948 ). 151

[w5c6b54-32] Role of Intravenous Albumin The indications of human albumin in the management of patients with decompensated cirrhosis have expanded in the last three decades. Albumin has both oncotic properties and as well as non-oncotic properties that make it an appealing agent in the treatment of HRS. Potential non-oncotic properties include scavenger, antioxidant and immunomodulatory effects. 42 , 152 While these pleiotropic effects are the focus of ongoing studies, it is important to note that these effects have not been corroborated in a large trial of hospitalized patients with decompensated cirrhosis. 153 , 154 Albumin’s main effect remains its ability to increase the effective circulating volume by increasing oncotic pressure, countering some of the physiologic maladaptation of portal hypertension. Studies since the 1990s have demonstrated that intravenous albumin decreases the risk of circulatory and kidney dysfunction post-paracentesis. 108 , 109 , 155 Albumin was later shown to significantly decrease the risk of kidney failure in the setting of SBP, where patients treated with albumin 1.5 g/kg on day 1 and 1 g/kg on day 3 had a significantly lower risk of renal impairment compared to those not receiving albumin (10% vs. 33%, respectively, p=0.002). 32 Albumin has been studied in other sub-populations of patients with decompensated cirrhosis and ascites, including in the outpatient (ANSWER and MACHT trials) and inpatient (ATTIRE trial) settings. 40 , 154 , 156 While the purpose of the current review is not to appraise the use of albumin in cirrhosis, as this has been extensively reviewed elsewhere, 157 , 158 the variable results around survival benefit in these studies does highlight the importance of (1) thoughtfully selected albumin dosing and (2) the need to adequately define which settings albumin use is best applied.

[w5c6b54-33] Albumin administration is recommended for the treatment of AKI in cirrhosis. 49 - 51 The current recommended dose of 1 g/kg/day for the first 48 hours from any AKI diagnosis theoretically improves any hypovolemia-induced component and serves as a diagnostic criterion for HRS-AKI. The current recommendation for treatment of HRS-AKI (once the diagnosis has been established) includes vasoconstrictors (as described above), with continued administration of albumin of 20-40g daily to help maintain effective circulating volume. It is important to highlight that the recommended dosage and duration of albumin are entirely based on expert opinion and represent extrapolation of our knowledge from studies in SBP and post-paracentesis. The evidence supporting albumin use in HRS is minimal: a small, non-randomized trial of terlipressin plus albumin versus terlipressin alone, in which patients treated with the combination therapy had higher response rates than in terlipressin monotherapy. 159 All contemporary trials of vasoconstrictors in HRS have been designed to include albumin in all treatment arms. Potential benefits of albumin need to be weighed against the risks of adverse events, specifically pulmonary edema (see above).

[w5c6b54-34] Fixed albumin dosing may not be appropriate for the management of HRS; tailored treatment based on intravascular volume status may be a better strategy instead. Some uncertainty remains as to how best to assess volume status. Parameters such as central venous pressure, static imaging like CT scan, and echocardiography are useful, when available. Serum albumin itself is not a marker of volume status, and therefore should not be used in the decision tree regarding albumin administration in HRS. The ATTIRE trial is a profound example demonstrating that targeting a specific serum albumin level does not prevent adverse outcomes in cirrhosis, as the inpatients in that trial who were transfused albumin to a serum target of 3 g/dL received more albumin (200 g vs. 20 g), did not see improvement in their primary outcome (29.7% vs. 30.2%), and suffered more pulmonary edema/fluid overload (28 vs. 8 patients). 154

[w5c6b54-35] Future research: Improving our assessment of volume status and adequacy of albumin administration should be a priority. Point-of-care ultrasound (POCUS) may provide additional information on volume status and therefore refine interventions beyond the “one-size-fits-all” approach. 160

[w5c6b54-36] Renal Replacement Therapy RRT should be considered to treat complications of kidney injury in cirrhosis that are no longer responsive to medical management alone. Morbidity and mortality are extremely high in patients with cirrhosis who require RRT, with short-term mortality exceeding 60-80% and low rates of recovery off dialysis in the absence of liver transplantation. 83 , 161 , 162 In the historical literature, HRS was once considered to have an untenable prognosis once starting dialysis, 163 , 164 though more contemporary literature suggests that HRS-AKI has similar outcomes to other forms of AKI (such as ATN) once starting RRT. 83 , 161 , 165 In both the AKI and CKD populations at large, there is equipoise around the optimal timing of RRT initiation. There is no clear mortality benefit when initiating RRT earlier using objective laboratory cutoffs compared to more general clinical assessments of volume overload, electrolyte imbalance, acidosis, or signs/symptoms of uremia. 166 - 170 There is little evidence examining thresholds for RRT initiation in cirrhosis, because this population has largely been excluded from the aforementioned trials. 171 Overall, the timing of RRT initiation in cirrhosis should be individualized and should consider both kidney and liver health trajectories.

[w5c6b54-37] Similarly, when considering modality of RRT, strong evidence again is lacking when comparing intermittent hemodialysis (iHD) to continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT). iHD is widely available, less expensive, and can effectively remove solutes/fluid in a shorter period of time, but is more likely to cause intradialytic hypotension in cirrhotic patients already prone to this due to low effective circulating volume. 172 CRRT is more expensive and can only be performed in the intensive care setting, but may be better tolerated due to more gradual fluid shifts and solute clearance. 173 Perhaps because of the critically ill population that requires it, CRRT is associated with higher mortality compared to iHD. 161 , 174 , 175 Peritoneal dialysis (PD) has theoretical advantages in cirrhosis, as it can concurrently drain ascites and may be better tolerated hemodynamically compared to either iHD or CRRT. However, there is limited data on its safety or efficacy in this population, and may also increase the risk of hypoalbuminemia (though albumin/protein loss in the dialysate) and peritontitis. 176

[w5c6b54-38] Of the many factors to weigh when considering RRT in cirrhosis, liver transplant eligibility is the most important. 161 , 171 , 177 - 179 Patients already listed for transplant or undergoing an expedited transplant evaluation should be initiated on RRT when clinically appropriate, with the goal of pre-operative optimization and bridging to transplant. Those in whom liver transplantation is not an option are more challenging, given their poor outcomes and high health care resource utilization. 180 Still, in the appropriate setting, a time-limited trial of RRT may be a reasonable offering, especially if there is a chance for an improvement in underlying synthetic liver function, a potential for change in transplant eligibility, or if initiating RRT will allow a patient time to make end-of-life arrangements. These clinical decisions can be burdensome on providers, so we advocate for (1) pro-active multidisciplinary discussions about the risks/benefits of RRT, potentially aided by a decision-support tool, 181 (2) consultation with palliative care services for symptom management and potential end-of-life planning, 182 , 183 and (3) frequent reassessment of clinical status within the context of the time-limited trial.

[w5c6b54-39] Extracorporeal liver support, which can remove non-dialyzable protein-bound substances such as bile acids and bilirubin, are sometimes discussed in the context of RRT in cirrhosis. 184 - 186 These modalities, which include proprietary devices using adsorbent columns, single pass albumin dialysis, and plasma exchange, have not been studied directly for the treatment of AKI, but rather for complications of decompensated cirrhosis, such as hepatic encephalopathy, or acute liver failure. 187 They are not widely available but are sometimes considered in clinical scenarios of dual liver and kidney failure.

[w5c6b54-40] Future research: While guidelines have called for increased palliative care involvement in decompensated cirrhosis in general, more research into the role of symptom-driven care and quality of life assessments are needed in those requiring RRT in cirrhosis. 188 Further work into optimizing RRT modalities are needed. Tools to facilitate clinical decision making and discussions between specialists and patients/caregivers would be welcomed.

[w5c6b54-41] Special Considerations for Terlipressin Liver Transplant Implications: Terlipressin has been associated with favorable renal outcomes around the time of liver transplant in several studies. Response to terlipressin is associated with improved 30-day and 90-day transplant-free survival, reduced need for RRT both pre- and post-LT, and a lower risk of developing CKD at one-year post-liver transplant. 128 , 189 - 191 Nevertheless, in listed patients, successful treatment with terlipressin lowers SCr and therefore lowers the MELD score, potentially increasing waitlist time, especially in patients with MELD >35. 192 , 193 Paradoxically, this improvement in renal function could lead to downgrading priority on the waitlist for patients being considered for liver transplant, which could lead to disagreement among patients and providers and limit the use of terlipressin despite its demonstrated benefit pre- and post-liver transplant. Several countries have adopted policy changes such as MELD exception points for HRS-AKI and weighted pre-treatment SCr and sodium to ensure waitlist priority in terlipressin-treated patients. 194 No such policy yet exists in the US; thus, the decision to use terlipressin in listed patients with MELD >35 should be individualized and account for regional variation in practice.

[w5c6b54-42] Acute on Chronic Liver Failure (ACLF): ACLF is a distinct clinical syndrome affecting patients with decompensated cirrhosis, characterized by intense systemic inflammation, extra-hepatic organ failures, and high short-term mortality. 27 ACLF is present in virtually all patients with HRS-AKI, and increasing severity is associated with decreased HRS reversal rates. 125 , 189 .

[w5c6b54-43] Alcohol-Associated Hepatitis (AH) and Systemic Inflammatory Response Syndrome (SIRS): Patients with AH are at high risk for developing AKI, which is a major predictor of mortality in this population. 195 , 196 Although the actual prevalence of HRS-AKI in AH is unknown, the pooled prevalence of AH in the 3 North American terlipressin trials was 33.7%, and patients with AH had higher rates of HRS reversal when treated with terlipressin compared to placebo (38.0 vs. 13.1%, p <0.001), as well as being more likely to meet the post hoc composite endpoint of being alive, RRT-free, and sustained HRS reversal on day 30 after treatment (33.9% vs 10.7%, p <0.001). 197 In the REVERSE trial, terlipressin-treated patients with SIRS had significantly higher HRS reversal rates compared to placebo-treated patients (42.9% vs. 6.7%, p = 0.0018) while no difference was seen in patients without SIRS (15.9% vs. 18.8%). 198 These results suggest terlipressin has potential to bridge to liver recovery in these populations.

[w5c6b54-44] Chronic Kidney Disease (CKD): There have been no formal studies evaluating HRS-AKI in patients with significant preexisting organic CKD, such as diabetic nephropathy or other parenchymal renal diseases, as these patients would have been excluded from terlipressin trials using ICA-HRS criteria due to underlying proteinuria or hematuria. However, extrapolating data from studies of HRS type 2, which reflects a more subacute and persistent form of functional kidney disease, there may be a role for terlipressin as well. In a retrospective analysis of 36 terlipressin-treated patients with HRS type 2, 62% had HRS reversal (SCr <1.5 mg/dL). 50% of these patients of HRS recurrence and were retreated with terlipressin with the majority achieving HRS reversal again (82%). 199 In another study of 52 terlipressin-treated patients with HRS type 2, 46% achieved HRS reversal and similarly, 50% had recurrence requiring retreatment, with 43% of these patients achieving a second HRS reversal. 200

[w5c6b54-45] Future Research: There is limited published data on the effects of transplant policy changes specific to terlipressin and HRS-AKI. 201 Thus, terlipressin’s effect on transplant allocation should be rigorously studied after its implementation in the United States to consider further policy changes. Further study of the link between systemic inflammation (in AH, ACLF, and SIRS) and terlipressin response may lead to new treatment insights and mechanistic discoveries. The role of terlipressin in patients with underlying structural kidney damage warrants further investigation.

[w5c6b54-46] Summary HRS-AKI is a rare and challenging complication of portal hypertension in patients with decompensated cirrhosis. Terlipressin should be used as a first-line treatment and initiated as early as possible once the diagnosis of HRS-AKI is established. Vasoconstrictors are one part of supportive medical care for this population, along with management of volume status, infection, and concurrent complications of kidney and liver failure. Terlipressin should be used with the primary goal of optimizing candidacy for liver transplantation, or in cases where this is not available, as a bridge to potential recovery of liver function. In that setting, the risks and benefits of vasoconstrictors like terlipressin should be thoughtfully assessed before initiating therapy. In the future, updates to the diagnostic criteria for HRS-AKI and identification of patients likely to respond to restoration of kidney perfusion will lead to improved outcomes and new therapies.

[w5c6b54-131] Major/standard criteria in regular type. Minor/additional criteria in italicized type. * Defined as partial response (regression of AKI stage with reduction of SCr to within 0.3 mg/dL of baseline value) or full response (return of SCr to within 0.3 mg/dL of baseline). ** Noted in these guidelines: “patients who fulfill these criteria may still have structural damage such as tubular damage. Urine biomarkers will become an important element in making a more accurate differential diagnosis between hepatorenal syndrome and acute tubular necrosis.”

[w5c6b54-134] Key: terli (telripressin), HRS (hepatorenal syndrome), SAE (serious adverse event), GI (gastrointestinal), SCr (serum creatinine) a Total row includes data from the integrated safety set (ISS) submitted to Food and Drug Administration Advisory Committee Meeting on July 15, 2020 (New Drug Application 22231), where available. Total number of patients in this set (349/249) differs slightly from the totals published in each clinical trial (356/256). Where ISS date was not available (enrollment creatinine, primary outcome, HRS reversal, baseline albumin, albumin given), a weighted average was calculated proportional to total number of patients in each trial (OT-0401 102/598 [18.7%], REVERSE 196/598 [32.8%], CONFIRM 300/598 [50.2%]). b Primary outcome was defined differently in each trial: OT-0401 (two consecutive SCr ≤1.5 mg/dL separated by 48 hours by day 14 of treatment without renal replacement therapy or transplant), REVERSE (two consecutive SCr ≤1.5 mg/dL separated by 40 hours by day 14 of treatment without renal replacement therapy or transplant), CONFIRM (two consecutive SCr ≤1.5 mg/dL separated by 2 hours by day 14 of treatment and subsequent 10 days of survival free of renal replacement therapy). c HRS Reversal defined as at least one SCr ≤1.5 mg/dL during treatment period. d Mortality was reported at 180 days for OT-0401 and at 90 days for REVERSE, CONFIRM, and Total. e Albumin given is reported while on treatment for OT-0401 and total dose (prior to and while on treatment) for REVERSE and CONFIRM.
